  • The two houses of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha along with the president of India comprises the Indian Parliament. Parliament is a pride of its citizens.

Passage of Bills Through Both Houses

  • When the legislation is summitted in the form of a bill passed by both houses of parliament and approved by the president, it becomes parliamentary law.
  • Parliament building or Sansad Bhavan is in New Delhi particularly the heart of India.
  • So the parliament is also present in the sansabhavan in New Delhi.
  • And in terms of a monetary bill, Lok Sabha mainly passes monetary bill.
  • But all the other bills are particularly passed by both houses of the parliament in conssonance with each other.
  • But with regard to the monetary bill or the money bill, Lok Sabha has more power in terms of the Rajya Sabha.

The Indian President: Election & Tenure

  • The Indian Presidency elected members of both houses of parliament, the legislative assembly of all states of India elect a president who will serve for 5 years.
  • That means the Indian president particularly serves for 5 years.
  • He’s the head of the state of India and the supreme commander of the Indian armed forces.
  • That means prime minister is a head of the country, head of the government in our country and the president is considered to be the head of the state.
  • The president’s executive power is exercised primarily by the prime minister and his council of ministers.
  • So definitely the president is a person who makes treaties, makes program, has more of a legislative power, makes signs, but the executive of the president’s power is actually done by the prime minister and his council of ministers.
  • Ram Nath Kovind is India’s 14th and current president.

Eligibility of the President

  • Any Indian citizen over the age of 35 who eligible to become a member of Lok Sabha is eligible for a presidential post.
  • He should not hold a prophet office under the government of India or any other state.
  • That means it clearly tells us one thing that a president cannot easily hold any position in the government if he’s the president.
  • Even before he becomes the president, he shouldn’t be holding a very high position in the government of India. Circumscribing to the powers of the president.

Duties & Powers of the President

  • Protecting the constitution and the laws of the land is a primary duty of the president of India.
  • He has a power to prorogue or summon both houses. He can also dissolve the Lok Sabha.
  • The bill can only become a law after it has been approved by the president.
  • So definitely this tells us one thing that the president has the supreme power and duty as well of the law of the land.
  • He can summon both houses that is both Lok Sabha and the Rajya Sabha. He can dissolve the Lok Sabha. How can he dissolve the Lok Sabha?
  • Because actually the members are elected by the people but the press but the power to dissolve them is with the president of India.

Assent of the President to Bills

  • A bill needs to be assented by the president and then it becomes a law.
  • That is why it has to be approved by the president.
  • The union cabinet along with the prime minister should advise the president in carrying out his duties.
  • So definitely even the president wants some help. So where will he take the help from?
  • From the prime minister and the council of ministers.
  • The president appoints the chief justice of the Supreme Court of India. the prime minister and other members of the council of ministers.
  • He can declare a war or conclude peace on the advice of the prime minister and his ministers.

Lok Sabha: Composition & Tenure

  • Coming to the look sabha members of the house of representatives or called as a house of people the Lok Sabha are directly elected by the people.
  • They will hold the position for 5 years or until Lok Sabha is disbanded by the president.
  • As it was said earlier as well, president can dissolve the Lok Sabha but Lok Sabha holds position only for five years.
  • Lok Sabha’s strength is 552 of which 530 are from the state.
  • So from different states of India as India being diversified.
  • 20 are from Union Territory and two are from the Anglo-Indian community commonly nominated by the president of India.
  • So people nominated from the Anglo-Indian community are also nominated by the president of India.
  • House membership is distributed to the states in proportion to the population.

Eligibility & No-Confidence Motions

  • Indian citizen at the age of 25 who listed on the electoral list anywhere in the country can only become the members of the Lok Sabha if they elected through universal adult suffrage.
  • Distrust resolutions against the government can only be introduced in par by the Lok Sabha.
  • Money bill can also be introduced only in the Lok Sabha.
  • As I’ve specified it earlier as well, money bill is introduced in the Lok Sabha and passed by particularly the Lok Sabha.
  • Though the advice of the Rajya Sabha is taken but the Rajya Sabha cannot deny the ruling or the policy of on money will by the Lok Sabha.
  • So in a general connotation in the general procedure of a bill the Rajya Sabha can keep the bill for 15 days and send its advisory note to the lok sabha that this is a policy or you know this is the amendment we want to make in the bill but in terms of money bill this cannot be done at all because it is a lo sabha who passes the money bill and the Rajya Sabha has can only give advice but cannot keep it for a very long time but it is also upon the Lok Sabha to take the advice or not.
  • Coming back to the Rajya Sabha, members of the Rajya Sabha indirectly elected by the state and territorial parliaments using single transferable voting.

Rajya Sabha: Members & Joint Sessions

  • The constitution states that the upper house can have up to 250 members and his term lasts for 6 years and one-third of the members retire every two years.
  • Join sessions between the two houses are very much rare.
  • The vice president of the country is Rajya Sabha’s exofficial chairman who presides over the session.
  • Any Indian citizen over the age of 30 whose name is on the voter list can be a member of the Rajya Sabha.
  • 12 members nominated by the president are not eligible to vote in the presidential election.

Representative Democracy & Ancient Traditions

  • Now you would certainly think about what was the Art Shastra and who wrote it.
  • Arthashastra was written by Kautilya and describes the monarchical governance in ancient India. how a ruler should rule, the king should follow the dharma, the well-being of people and other such representatives.
  • Now it becomes important to understand certain other dimensions.

Early Charters & the Governor of Bengal

  • Even without a nation state, our experiments on representative governance began in the 6th century BC in the form of the Republic of the former kingdoms of Pava, Kushinara, Riti, Kapilavastu and Rama, Subuta, Mela, Sunsam, Migiri, Pipali, Kolanga.
  • The modern parliament, cabinet and prime minister was sabha, samiti and ganapati of these republics.
  • So how varied it is right in today’s contemporary scenario we regard a prime minister to be prime minister and the president to be the president of India.
  • But in those times you used to call them subas and sumis in the gapati.
  • So how varied another dimension the emperors in these so-called republics were bound by the collective wisdom of their advisors in the form of council of ministers.
  • Their ruler was a monarch bound by dharma a concept like today’s constitutionalism.
  • Dharma is essentially a part of political justice or political ethics or duty and responsibility in which the ruler was bound.

The Westminster Model & Indian Bodies

  • It is very simple to assume that the entire Westminster model was shipped directly to India and imposed on us and we did not experiment with the process first but it was an organic process.
  • Indian agencies and deliberative bodies evolved every few decades especially from 1601 to 1935.
  • All of these were stratified at the micro level with autonomous bodies such as Gram Sabha and the Panchayat. Now coming to the charter acts.
  • The 1601 Charter Act in which the governor and East India Company approved to create, enact and compose such a large number of laws, constitutions, orders, ordinances appears to be necessary and convenient for a good government.
  • So laid the first foundation of the modern legislature process in India.
  • The governors and council of the three presidents were first legislated by the 1726 charter. Moving ahead to the 1773 regulatory act.
  • The beginning of the East India Company’s parliamentary system for government was characterized by the 1773 regulatory act.
  • The process of territorial integration and centralization of administration in India is said to have been initiated by this law.

The Charter Acts: Bengal Presidency & the 1833 Act

  • It gave supremacy to the Bengal presidency and the governor of Bengal was appointed as the governor. Moving ahead, the 1833 Charter Act.
  • Under the 1833 Charter Act, the company’s trading rights were terminated and it was nothing more than an administrator body of the Indian crown.
  • Governor General of Bengal was then appointed as a governor general of India and empowered to govern the entire British India. Now comes the 1853 Charter Act.
  • According to the 1853 Charter Act, the bill went through usual three stages and was then referred to a special committee.

Verbal Council Discussions & Legislative Powers

  • The council discussions became verbal rather than written when acting on the legislative abilities.
  • The new council became more than just legislative and became the epidome of representative council gather to investigate and correct dissatisfaction.
  • The government of India act of 1858 India’s first struggle for independence that is the 1857 rebellion and we also call this as the first Indian independence protest or the act.
  • It accelerated the process of establishing an appropriate representative government.
  • Under the government of India act of 1858 Britain began participating in informal councils.
  • The Indian Council Act of 1892 granted both the central council and the state council the right to discuss the budget for the first time under certain conditions.
  • However, members of the council did not yet have the authority to submit or propose resolutions to split the council on financial debates.
  • Interestingly and importantly, the 1892 Council Act gave the privilege of asking questions and questioning government members for the first time.
  • The first question was asked on February 16th, 1893.

Questions in the Council & the 1909 Reforms

  • This was asked by Maharaja Binga and the question was about the difficulties caused by the system of gathering supply provision for government officials on the tour. The Government of India Act 1990.
  • The system of diarchy was introduced by the Government of India Act 1990.
  • The law effectively and broadly initiated the separation of powers between the center and the state.
  • The system established dual government firms in each of the eight states.
  • Control of several areas of government called the transferred list was given to the government of the Indian ministers who were answerable to the state legislature.
  • At the same time all other areas of government that is a reserve list remained under the control of the viseroy.

The Government of India Act, 1935

  • The Indian governance act of 1935.
  • Under the Indian governance act of 1935 two chambers of the Bengal Parliament were established.
  • The Legislative Council and the Legislative Assembly.
  • The assembly consisted of 250 members with a total of 63 or more and 65 or less counelors on April 9th 1937.
  • Therefore, after independence, India developed as a parliamentary democracy.
  • With a clear separation of powers among the three bodies that is the legislature, the executive and the judiciary which we come to know as the three organs of the government of India.
  • The judiciary reviews, the executive implements and the legislature establishes the policy.

  • So we still believe in the representative form of democracy with the parliamentary system of government with Lok Sabha and the Rajya Sabha being superior and the constitution being supreme.

Merits: Harmony Between Lok Sabha & Rajya Sabha

  • A few subtopics before we’ve already learned about what is a parliamentary form of government in India.
  • What are the various components of it pertaining to the concept of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha?

Harmony Between Executive & Legislature

  • Now the first merit of the parliamentary form of government pertains to the parliament or the legislature in India maintains a harmony between executive and legislature.
  • There is close harmony and cooperation between the executive branch and the legislature.
  • Since ministers belong to the ruling party and are the majority party in the legislature, it is not so difficult to get the legislature support and approval for government policies and programs.
  • Therefore, there is less conflict between the executive branch and legislature.
  • The second most important merit is that parliament is responsible and clean.
  • The council of ministers is solely responsible for the legislature.
  • But individual ministers are also individually responsible for their omission and duties.

Opposition as a Watchdog of Democracy

  • They try to stay clean opposition which is also considered as a watchdog of democracy wherein media is also concerned to be called as a watchdog of democracy.
  • So you know even in cases if you know at some point in time you’re writing something as a hypothetical example if you’re writing something and nobody gives you a value point and nobody criticizes you in that case you will not be able to know what is wrong in my ideology. you’ll not be able to know what is wrong in my writing.
  • But if somebody gives you value points and somebody gives you a criticism, you will work upon it in the same manner in the parliamentary form of government where there’s a majority party as well as there is an opposition party. The opposition party acts in the same manner.
  • It criticizes the majority party in terms of its ruling which also makes a democracy more powerful and parliamentary form of government acts as a balanced system where the ruling party cannot consider itself as a superior because somebody is there to keep a check on the majority party.
  • So thus it is also called as a guard dog of the government.
  • It’ll expose corrupt and inefficient governments.
  • The third merit of parliamentary form of government is quick decision making.
  • Lord Bri praised the parliamentary government for its ability to make swift decisions.

Quick Decisions by a Majority Government

  • The ruling party has a majority support in the legislature.
  • So it can make quick decisions in response to contingencies. Absolutely.
  • When the ruling party has enough of support and it has come in majority then all the pillars of the parliamentary form of government will be in their support. Nobody will be there to oppose.
  • But in case if it’s different in form of a coalition, it will be very different in terms of the ruling party.

Flexibility

  • The next merit of parliamentary form of government is its flexibility.
  • Parliamentary governments have a lot of flexibility. in dealing with changing circumstances and even emergencies.
  • The system is flexible and easy to adapt to new realities.
  • One cabinet can be replaced with a new one without much controversy to deal with serious situations.
  • The next merit of parliamentary form of government is that it is conducive to national integration.
  • Parliamentary governments help promote national integration while trying to address the concerns of different regions and cultures of the country.
  • Now we clearly know one aspect that India is a diversified country to bring all people in parallels is very difficult.
  • Thus parliamentary governments help promote national integration because both the Rajya Sabha and the Lok Sabha have diversified population in terms of MPs and MLAs.
  • Member of parliaments belong to different states of India altogether promoting thus diversity.
  • The next merit of parliamentary form of government being its educational value.

Discussion of Policies & Question Hour

Political Education of the People

  • Thus, parliamentary governments are of great value in terms of people’s political education.

Demerits of the Parliamentary Form of Government in India

Video Part 1 · starts at 7:04:30

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • At the end of this session, you will be able to understand about the demerits of parliamentary form of government and you’ll also be able to understand about the comparison between parliamentary and presidential system.

Demerits of the Parliamentary System

  • Now a few subtopics earlier in module three of the course in comparative government and politics we learned and understood about the merits about what is parliamentary form of government how did it evolve in India where was it adopted from so as we all know the Indian constitution is an imitated one it has been adopted from various other constitutions and so is the concept of parliamentary form of government in India.
  • Now firstly pertaining to the parliamentary form of government and its demerits.
  • Firstly, parliamentary form of government is a unique and dynamic concept particularly in India.
  • Before understanding its demerits, we should also know what is parliamentary form of government.
  • Parliamentary itself, the term is self-explanatory.
  • That is where the concept of parliament or as we call it the legislature and the constitution form a very component form a very important component in India.

Lok Sabha & Rajya Sabha Tenure

  • In the parliamentary form of government there are two houses in India that is the Lok Sabha the house of the people and the Rajya Sabha the council of states.
  • Lok Sabha is appointed for five years while Rajya Sabha is appointed for six years.
  • So even if one house that is Lok Sabha gets dissolved due to any non-adjournment motion, Rajya Sabha is at least present in that instantaneous point.
  • Now parliamentary form of government is very dissimilar to the presidential form of government.
  • In the presidential form of government, the president is considered to be supreme.
  • Then the constitution and there is no parliament per se, a constitutional body is not present at all.

Weak Separation of Powers

  • Now pertaining to the demerits of it, firstly there is weak separation of powers.
  • This system violates the principle of separation of powers.
  • Since ministers are members of the ruling party and the coalition, they control policymaking.
  • But in principle, policymaking is a territory of the legislature.

Cabinet Dictatorship

  • The second principle being the cabinet dictatorship.
  • The council of ministers tends to be authoritarian and irresponsible. with the support of most of the House of Representatives.
  • It also has little respect for the legislature as a majority are obliged to support the government.
  • In this way, the cabinet government is reduced to a party government and parliamentary democracy turns into a cabinet dictatorship.
  • Further moving on, there’s a failure to take prompt decisions.
  • The council of ministers are also called as a cabinet or advisers to the press prime minister in the parliamentary form of government has no fixed term and cannot adopt bold and long-term policies.
  • The problem is often exacerbated by unstable coalition governments. Coalition partners tend to fight between them. As a result, they cannot adopt bold policies.
  • According to AV DIY, private governments cannot take swift, bold and effective measures in the event of a crisis or war.

Difficulty in Convincing Cabinet & Legislature

  • The prime minister takes time to convince his cabinet colleagues to decide.
  • Similarly, it is not easy for the government to convince the legislature to make swift decisions to manage the crisis. Another demerit is partisanship.
  • In the parliamentary system, political parties are driven by partisan motivation rather than national or national interest.
  • The ruling party rarely sees the benefit of criticizing the opposition, but the opposition often opposes the government of the opposition.
  • Now we all understand how important is the opposition in any country.
  • If there is no individual to criticize your piece of work, how will you actually have a great analysis in terms of what is right and what is the wrong path?
  • Thus, the crux of the ideology is that whenever there’s an opposing party, it is better because the government, the ruling party actually comes to know what are its fault findings. The next year I read government by amateurs.

Amateur Ministers

  • Parliamentary governments lack the ability and effectiveness because most of the ministers are amateurs.
  • Since the minister must be appointed from among the members of the legislature, the scope of appointing talented people as minister is limited.
  • Often important considerations in appointing ministers are cast, religion, community factions and influence within the party rather than skills, abilities and talents controlled by bureaucracy.
  • The cabinet is powerful but the more powerful are the bureaucrats.
  • Ministers, mostly amateurs, rely on civil servants for expert advice and guidance.
  • Civil servants use their true power in the name of the minister. It does not come out to the front.
  • They are not responsible for the legislature. and civil servants in the parliamentary form of government are referred to as permanent executives while the ministers in the parliamentary form of government are referred to as political executives.
  • It is a minister who’s responsible for the decision made by the civil servant in the name of it and this particularly leads to irresponsibility and red tapism.

Other Flaws: Tenure & Accountability

  • It can also be vice versa.
  • In certain times, parliamentary government has certain flaws as well.
  • In particular, huge partisanships, lack of expertise, and the difficulty of making quick decisions in times of crisis are obstacles. But the main benefit is accountability.
  • The government is responsible for the legislature especially the popular house of representatives.

Democratic Responsiveness vs Presidential Stability

  • In other words, the parliamentary government is relatively democratic than the presidential government.
  • Now this is a basic difference between the parliamentary and the presidential form of government and the basis on which it is divided.
  • If we take the basis of executive then in parliamentary it is dual executive that is bureaucracy and ministers. In presidential there is a single executive.
  • In terms of accountability, executives are accountable to the legislature while in presidential executives are not accountable to the legislature.
  • Ministers are only from the member of parliament while in presidential system people outside the legislature can also be appointed as ministers. Dissolution of the lower house.
  • The prime minister can dissolve before the expiry of the term in the parliamentary form of government while the presidential form the parliament the president and the parliament cannot be dissolved.
  • In terms of tenure in parliamentary there is no fixed tenure while in presidential we have a fixed tenure. task.
  • Through this learner difference between parliamentary and presidential, we actually come to understand which is more accountable and beneficial to adopt.
  • Though both are in terms very secure and sound but it becomes imperative to explain that in terms of India being democratic for quite a long time parliamentary government of India has been successful and will be successful in future times.

Meaning of the Presidential System (United States)

Video Part 1 · starts at 7:16:03

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • At the end of this session, you will be able to understand the presidential system, the concept by Alexander Hamilton, president powers in United States, the legislature and judiciary in United States and the check and balance system in the United States with the advantages of the democratic presidential system.

Presidential vs Parliamentary Systems

  • In comparison to presidential system, there is the parliamentary form of government where the prime minister and Indian constitution is considered to be supreme with the two houses of Lok Sabha involving in it that is Lok Sabha or the house of the people and the Rajya Sabha that is council of states.
  • Now firstly circumscribing to the presidential system.
  • Some representative and constitutional democracy has a presidential system based on the concept of separation and sharing of power among the three independent and coordinated government sectors of legislation, administration and judiciary.
  • You also understand that the parliamentary system of government also has separation of powers between legislature, executive, judiciary, mainly the three organs.
  • But the fourth organ is considered to be the media.
  • The United States is a founder and prime example of the presidential system and is the only model used in several other democracies such as Argentina, Brazil, Mexico and the Philippines.
  • The presidential system unlike the parliamentary democracy has a strong and independent chief executive with broad authority related to domestic affairs or both domestic and foreign policy.

The President’s Independence from the Legislature

  • The president’s independence from the legislature is based on elections by those who are directly responsible for the president rather than the legislature as in the parliamentary system.
  • Now just beside these two points you can see a clear picture of the parliamentary system.
  • This is a president house in India and is a photographic picture of the republic day republic day parade where the horses are giving a big salute to the flag in the president’s house.
  • Opposing to the parliamentary system, Alexander Hamilton in the 70th paper of the federalist papers claimed a strong presidency as approved by the US Constitution.

Administrative Energy & Defence of the Community

  • He wrote that administrative energy is a protagonist of a good government definition.
  • It is essential to protect the community from foreign attacks.
  • It is not less important for the steady management of the law, property protection, freedom and security against businesses and ambitions, factions and anarchy. The president powers in the United States.

The President as Chief Executive

  • In the US presidential system, the president is a chief executive of the government and the head of the state.
  • The president oversees the cabinet or the heads of various executive departments and the administrative departments of the government including various administrative offices and institution.
  • Beside these two points, you can see how powerful is other presidential powers.
  • Validity with a cluster of stars and a cluster of strong eagle with two symbols in hand.
  • This represents a very strong hegemonic power which United States is in terms of its military in terms of its social and political power.
  • The chief executive and subordinate executive officers have the authority and obligation to enforce the law and to control the day-to-day operations of the government.
  • In particular, the president commands the army and is responsible for the defense of the country against domestic turmoil and foreign attacks.
  • Moving further, commanding the legislature and the judiciary in the United States, independent and separate legislatures and judiciaries that share power with the executive branch prevent the powerful executive branch of the democratic presidential system from becoming excessive or abusive.

The US Bicameral Legislature & Judiciary

  • A bicameral parliament consisting of the house of representatives and the senate comprises the US government.
  • The judicial department that interprets and applies the law in certain cases includes the supreme court, the interim court of appeals and the entrylevel district court of jurisdiction.
  • In a similar manner, the judiciary in India has district/revenue courts.
  • The next at the hierarchy is the high court and at the apex level is a supreme court which commands the most authority in the judiciary in India.

Checks & Balances Among Three Branches

  • How does the check and balance system in United States work?
  • There’s a check and balance between three separate branches of the government which like parliamentary democracy prevents any branch from continuing to control the government.

  • In contrast to parliamentary democracy which allows elections each time the government loses majority support in parliament.

  • The House of Representatives may force a colleague to leave the office due to unethical criminal behavior, though this too happens rarely.
  • Citizens usually have no way to force an unpopular president out of power or change members of parliament prior to scheduled regular elections.
  • Therefore, the main legislative and administrative officer presidential democracy are less accountable to the public than parliamentary people.

Advantages of the Presidential System

Regional Governors & Councils

  • The regional governor is usually appointed by the royal family and oversees one or more city councils. half of which are appointed and half are elected.
  • The governor along with the council is responsible for functions such as finance, health, education, agriculture and local government.
  • The principle of consultation works at all levels of government including village and tribal governance.
  • In terms of the council, these councils discuss state needs work on development budgets, consider future development plans, and monitor ongoing projects. the governor and the vice governor of each state chair and vice chair the respective state legislatures.
  • Each council consists of at least 10 private citizens like Majlis al-Shura members of the council participate in committees that focus in a variety of issues of interest to the state.
  • The council issues a report that is submitted to the minister of interior and then passed to the appropriate government ministry for consideration.

King Fahd’s 1992 Bylaws & the 13 Provinces

  • The state legislature system is a result of the bylaws enacted by King Fahd in 1992.
  • These bylaws divided the country into 13 provinces defining the management structure, management methods, responsibilities of the governor and other regional officers.
  • In 1993, King Fad nominated 210 members to the provincial councils.
  • In 2005, city elections were held for half of each member of the 178 city councils in the kingdom.
  • The other half of the members and the mayor are appointed.

  • Many of these Celtic rulers either formed an alliance or fall to the Romans who made Britannia nearly modern England and Wales as part of the empire.

  • The angels, the saxons and judes settled in England, formed many kingdoms. Seven of them are called the seven kingdoms.

The Wars of the Roses & the Lancastrians

  • However, the term can be a bit misleading as it does not truly convey the complex political composition of Britain at the time.
  • Henry 4 was a grandson of Edward 3 and the son of John of Gaunt, Duke of Lancaster.
  • Therefore, his dynasty was known as a Lancaster family.
  • During most of his reign, Henry 4 was forced to fight plots and rebellions.
  • Part of his success was due to the military power of his son. and the future Henry Wi’s own reign which began in 1413 was largely free from domestic conflicts and the king was free to wage a 100red years war in France in which he won the conquest.
  • His sudden death in 1422 left his little son Henry 6 on the throne, giving the French the opportunity to overthrow British rule.

Unpopular Regency & Weak Leadership

  • The unpopularity of Henry’s regent and subsequently Henry’s own ineffective leadership led to the weakening of the Lancaster family.
  • The end of the war of roses formed a major turning point in the history of monarchy.
  • Many aristocrats were decimated in the battlefield or executed for participation in the war and many aristocratic nations were lost to the crown.
  • In addition, feudalism was dying and the feudal army dominated by the baron became obsolete.
  • Therefore, the Tudor monarchs easily reestablish absolute hegemony within the territory, ending the conflict with the aristocrats who plague the former monarchs.
  • Coming further to the Scottish monarchy, in Scotland, like Britain, a monarchy emerged after the withdrawal of Rome in the early 5th century.
  • The three groups that lived in Scotland at this time were the pigs who lived in Kingdom of Pictavia, the Britons who lived in several kingdoms in southern Scotland and the Scotty of Dal rather.
  • The expansion of Scottish territory continued over the next two centuries as other territories such as the Scraihide were conquered or acquired by dynasty marriage.
  • Early Scottish monarchs did not inherit the crown directly.

Rival Dynasties

  • The monarchy changed between two and sometimes three branches of the Alpine family.
  • However, as a result, the lines of rival dynasties collided often violently. The Republican phase.
  • Elizabeth’s death in6003 brought the end of Tudor rule.

James VI & the Union of Crowns

  • Since she had no children, she was succeeded by the Scottish monarch James 6, whose maternal great grandmother was Henry 8’s elder sister.
  • James the 6 ruled England as James one after what was known as a union of the crowns.
  • England and Scotland were in the personal union under one monarch.
  • But James Swan became the first monarch to call himself the king of England in6004.

  • Disputes were often related to issues of royal and parliamentary powers, especially the part to impose taxes.
  • Around 1642 when the English civil war began the conflict between the king and parliament culminated.

The Act of Union (1707)

  • The act of union of 1707 after Anna joined the succession issue reappeared shortly thereafter.
  • Scottish estates were furious that the British parliament did not consult with them about choice of Sophia of Hanover.
  • They passed security legislation and threatened to end the personal union between England and Scotland.
  • The English Parliament retaliated under the Alien Act 1705 and threatened to ruin the Scottish economy by reducing free trade.
  • The Scottish estate agreed to act of union 1707 under which England and Scotland were integrated into a single kingdom of Great Britain and succession was determined under the rule stipulated by the reconciliation act.

The Monarchy in Ireland & the Papal Grant

  • The monarchy in Ireland in the 12th century Pope Adrian IV awarded Henry 2 of England papul which allowed him to own Ireland.
  • This is because the Irish Christian Church at that time did not follow the Roman Catholic Church and had various heretical beliefs.
  • So the pope wanted the British monarch to annex Ireland and bring the Irish church into the Catholic Church.
  • The Pope gave Ireland to the king of England as a feudal territory and the nominal overlord of the pope.
  • Dermat fled to England and asked Henry for help.
  • Kendry refused but he agreed to allow a group of Anglo nominous trits and adventures led by Richard declar Earl of Pembroke to assist him to reign the throne.
  • Now how did it lead to the empire to commonwealth?

The Crown & the British Empire

  • Previously the crown operated the entire British Empire as a single organization.
  • The British crown was considered a single crown and was subordinate to all Dominions and colonies.
  • The Statute of Westminster was passed in 1931, dominating the right of the London government to advise the king on actions to be taken with respect to individual dominions.
  • As a result, the single British crown that operated throughout the empire was replaced by a separate crown for each dominion.
  • Therefore, the monarchy system is no longer limited to Britain and certain British monarchies existed only within the jurisdiction of Britain, the crown, the UK.

  • The British monarchy was hereditary and the absence of hair to the throne. The monarchy was the eldest son or daughter.
  • The succession to the British throne act changed the law of succession to the British throne because it replace men’s favorite primarch with absolutely primogenitor.
  • In other words, the first born will come first regardless of gender.
  • The law also ended the disqualification of those married to Roman Catholics from the succession. the part of minox today in contemporary scenario at first glance the crown is purely ritual and appears to have little effect on the reality of government politics and power but it still retains realistic and important power even if it is not exercised by convention.

Government in the Name of the Crown

  • Power is still exercised by the monarch provides a guarantee against extreme government.

  • Moreover, the queen stands as a factor in national unity and cohesion, especially during crisis such as natural disaster, conflicts, and terrorist attacks.

Continuity & Ritual in Modern Britain

  • British history is characterized by continuity and one of the key aspects of British societies, political institutional permanence.
  • In the 19th century, many formal rituals were devised to give the impression of a timeless tradition to reassure the era of radical change.
  • She does not play the role of her executive, but the queen still has a right to be informed, advised, and warned.

  • The Constitution gives Congress the sole power to declare war, the right to confirm or reject many presidential appointments and substantive investigative powers.
  • The House of Representatives is made up of 435 elected members divided into 50 states in proportion to the total population.
  • Moving further, in addition, there are six non- voting members representing the District of Colombia, the Federal Republic of Puerto Rico, four other regions of the United States that is US Samoa, Guam,

The US House of Representatives

  • US Virgin Islands and Northern Mariana Islands.

  • He or she is the third in succession to the president.
  • Members of the House of Representatives are elected every two years, must be 25 years old, should be a US citizens for at least 7 years, and beside any representative state, not necessarily a district represented by them.
  • The House of Representatives is exclusively assigned several powers including the inauguration of revenue bills, impeachment of few federal authorities, the election of the president if the electoral colleges are tired.

The US Senate

  • The Senate is also a very dynamic aspect of the United States Legislature.
  • The Senate is made up of 100 senators, two in each state.
  • Until the 17th amendment was ratified in 1913, senators were elected by the state legislature rather than by a general vote.
  • Since then, they have been elected by the people of each state for a six-year term.
  • Senators are staggering and about one-third of the Senate will be reelected every two years. Senators must be at least 30 years old.
  • Must be US citizens for at least 9 years and must reside in the state represented by the senator.

Role of the Vice President & Senate Powers

  • The vice president of the United States serves as president of the Senate and can vote decisively if there’s a tie in the Senate.
  • The Senate has the sole authority to confirm the appointment of a president who requires consent and to provide advice and consent to ratify the treaty. However, there are two exceptions to this rule.
  • The House of Representatives must also approve a treaty that includes the appointment of the vice president and foreign trade.
  • The Senate also attempts an impeachment case of federal authorities referred to by the House of Representatives.
  • Both the House of Representatives and the Senate must pass the same bill with the majority in order to pass it and send it to the president for signature.
  • If the president rejects the bill, at least two-thirds of the agencies can veto it by voting in favor of and repassing the bill each meeting room. Now what is the legislative process of USA?
  • The first step in the legislative process is to submit the bill to Congress.
  • Some important bills are traditionally introduced at the request of the president like for example the annual federal budget.

Committees & the Passage of Bills

  • After the bill is submitted, it will be referenced and reviewed by the appropriate committees.
  • 17 Senate committees, 70 subcommittees, 23 House committees and 104 subcommittees.
  • Each committee oversees a particular policy area and subcommittees undertake more specialized policy areas.
  • For example, the House Committee on Methods and Means includes a subcommittee on social security and trade.
  • The bill may first be considered by a subcommittee where it may be fully approved, amended or rejected.
  • If a member of the subcommittee agrees to proceed with the bill, the bill will be reported to the entire committee where the process will be repeated again.
  • Throughout this stage of the process, committees and subcommittees call hearings to investigate the strengths and weaknesses of the bill.
  • They can invite experts, supporters and opponents to the committee, provide testimony and harness the powerson is needed.
  • If the entire committee votes for approval of the bill, it will be reported to the House or Senate floor and most leaders will decide when to put the bill on the calendar for consideration.

Congress & Legislative Authority

  • Congress is one of the three equivalent branches of the government is given significant authority by the constitution.
  • All government legislative powers are granted to Congress.
  • In short, it is the only part of government that can enact new legislation or modify existing legislation.
  • The executive branch issues the rules at the full power of the law.
  • But these are only under the authority of the law enacted by Congress.
  • The president can reject legislation passed by Congress.
  • But Congress can also override veto with two-thirds of the votes in both the Senate and House of Representatives.
  • Article one of the constitution lists the powers of parliament and the specific areas in which it may be legislated.

Russia’s Federal Assembly & the Duma

  • Now how does this legislative system of Russia look like?

  • The 166 seat federal council consists of regional leaders. Duma oversees economic problems.
  • The federal council coordinates the exchange between national and local governments.

A Weak Russian Legislature

  • The Russian legislature is weak.
  • The president has almost all constitutional powers.
  • The general assembly has a power to discuss issues.
  • But its power to influence decision is questionable.
  • According to the constitution, if Duma voted against the president’s prime minister’s choice three times, Duma would have to dissolve. Now, how does a Duma parliament look like? It is more influential in the two houses.
  • The name Duma was first used by the Tsars to represent the lower house which was forced to disband on the closing days to SARS Russia.

The Federation Council Under Putin

  • In the 1990s, a federation council consisted of the governors of 89 local governments and the heads of local councils where Russia was divided.
  • In 2000, Putin strengthened the federal council’s control by introducing legislation that changed the composition of the House of Councilors from local elected civil servants to members appointed by Moscow.
  • The Federation Council generally complies with the wishes of Russian leaders.
  • Its members are appointed by the regional council.
  • The Russian legislative body was established by a constitution approved by a referendum in December 1993.
  • The first election to the federal assembly was held at the same time.
  • Under the constitution, members elected in December 1993 were called provisional because they had a term of only two years.

Powers & Responsibilities of the US President

Legal System – United States vs Russia

Video Part 2 · starts at 2:18:21

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • At the end of this session, you will be able to define the legal system of United States.
  • You’ll also be able to describe the legal system of Russia.

Judicial Systems: United States vs Russia

  • In the previous two subtopics, we learned and understood about how the legislative system and the executive system of United States and Russia works in.
  • What are the important recommendations as to how the two states, two countries work upon the legislature and the executive itself are very important components of any legislative system.
  • Thus to learn and understand about the legal system of United States and Russia, it becomes imperative to understand its consequence.
  • The legal system of United States is an impact of precedent.

Stare Decisis & the Common Law

  • The principle of stair decises.
  • The common law definition principle is that courts follow the decisions of higher level courts within the same jurisdiction.
  • It is from this legacy of gay decisions that a somewhat predictable and consistent legal system has emerged. The court hierarchy.
  • The level or hierarchy of a court largely defines the extent to which a decision made by one court has a binding impact on another.
  • For example, the federal court system is based on a three tired structure in which the US district court is a trial level court.
  • The United States Court of Appeal is a first level court of appeal and the US Supreme Court is a final arbitrator of the law.

Jurisdiction & Common Law in the US

  • The term jurisdiction has two important meanings in American law.
  • The term common law may refer to any of the following. common law as differentiated from civil law.
  • The American system is a common law system and relies heavily on case law informal judgments.
  • In our common law system, even if statues are at stake, the judicial decisions in the previous proceedings are very important to the court’s resolution of the previous proceedings.
  • The civil law system does not rely on case law, but on code that explicitly provides the rules for determining more specific disputes.
  • If a judge needs to go beyond the letters of the code in dealing with the dispute, the judge’s resolution is non-binding and probably not even relevant to subsequent decisions involving other parties.
  • Common law may refer to judge created law, also known as case law.
  • A proceeding is a legal decision based on a series of specific facts involving a party who has a real interest in a dispute.

Case Law as Decisional Law

  • Pure decisional case law.
  • The court sought to decide the case based on previous court decisions, case law and or policy and an inherent sense of impartiality.
  • In the case of pure decision law, there are no applicable laws or constitutional provisions.
  • This type of decision is called a judicial doctrine.
  • Historically, the term case law has referred to certain areas of law to property etc. that were created by judges or began as pure decision making. Case law is based in constitutional provisions.

Judicial Review & Advisory Opinions

  • The court sought to consider whether certain legislation and government actions were in line with the US Constitution or certain state constitutions.
  • The American judicial system, the courts had the power to make decisions.
  • Therefore, federal courts are prohibited issuing advisory opinions or opinions without examples.
  • Threshold issues designed to preclude advisory opinions.
  • Given the federal courts ban the advisory opinion, there are certain threshold prerequisites that must be met before the federal court hears the case.
  • The main prerequisites for court review are standing.
  • The parties must have a real recognizable usually financial or proprietary interest in the proceedings. finality.
  • In the case of an appeal or agency review, the proceedings by the court of first instance of the administrative body must be final and must have a real impact on the parties.

The Russian Civil Law System

  • Coming to the legal system of Russia, the Russian Federation has a civil law system.
  • The Russian law includes both codified law and other laws that in principle must be consistent with the relevant law. Legislation is the highest jurisdiction.
  • However, the constitutional court can invalidate unconstitutional laws.

Case Law in Russia

  • Case law can be recognized as a de facto source of law.
  • This means that decisions by the constitutional court including the interpretation of the law are binding on all courts, state agencies, legal entities, individuals.
  • The Supreme Court’s ruling is also binding on lower courts to ensure the uniformity of legal affairs. The court structure and hierarchy in Russia.
  • The Russian court structure consists of a constitutional court or federal court and district court, both of which are headed by the Supreme Court.
  • Federal courts include the commercial courts that settle economic disputes and the general courts that handle all other cases.
  • District courts include the magistrates court which resolves minor issues in the constitutional statuto courts of the Russian region which handle cases late in the regional constitution decree.
  • The constitutional court deals with dispute over the constitutionality of certain legal acts and is a separate jurisdictionary in the Russian court system.
  • Now one perhaps dynamic notification is that the legal system of United States is very different from the American judicial system and the legal system of Russia is very different from both the legal and executive system of United States.

The House of Lords: Spiritual & Temporal Members

  • Its members have not been elected by the general public.

Evolution of Parliament

  • Parliament evolved from an early medieval council that advised sovereigns in England and Scotland.
  • The Queen of Parliament is often said to be a fully sovereign authority. But such a position is controversial.
  • In modern times, true power is given to the House of Commons.

  • Now what is the history of the British parliament which pertains to in the middle ages and early modern times there were three kingdoms within the British Isles England, Scotland and Ireland.

  • The act of 1707 brought Britain and Scotland under the parliament of the United Kingdom and the act of 1800 included Ireland under the parliament of the United Kingdom.

  • In 1066, William of Normandy introduced a feudal system.
  • In 1215, Nayano’s parcel secured Magna Carta from John.

Simon de Montfort & the First Elected Parliament

  • In 1265, the sixth Earl of Lancaster, Simon de Montfort, summoned the first elected parliament.
  • The parliamentary election franchise for country members is uniform across the country reaching an annual rent of 40 shillings for all who own land.
  • In the autonomous terrain, franchisee varied across the country.
  • There were various arrangements in each autonomous region.

The Model Parliament of 1295

  • It was a settling for the so-called model parliament of 1295 adopted by Edward 1.
  • By the reign of Edward 3, the parliament was divided into two homes.
  • One included aristocrats and higher clergy and the other included knights and citizenship.
  • Here without the consent of both houses in sovereign no law could be enacted or taxes could be levied.
  • The Welsh Act of 153542 annexed Wales as part of England and brought representatives of Wales to Parliament.

The Speaker, Debate & the Legislative Programme

  • The government’s main task at governments is to implement a legislative program in which the government fought and won the final general election.
  • At the beginning of each new session of parliament, the House of Representatives elects speakers from its members.
  • Speakers preside over and regulate debates and rules regarding the points of order and action of members.
  • Speakers do not participate in discussions or votes just to break the tie.
  • This is when you force a speaker to vote in favor of the status quo.
  • Encouraging members to speak in debate is entirely in the hands of the speaker.

Bills, the Prime Minister & Party Leadership

  • Due to constitutional customs that were not enacted until the 20th century, the prime minister is always a member of the House of Commons and not a member of the House of Lords.
  • Political parties appoint leaders of the House of Commons to oversee the party’s legislative program except for occasional independence.
  • Both government and opposition members are under the party control within the governments and their discipline is exercised by members called whips especially with respect to voting.
  • The tradition of having to read the bill three times in the comments and before it is voted on is based on the need to give members enough time to investigate the underlying principles and details of the bill.

Readings of a Bill & the Committee Stage

  • The first reading is purely formal while the second reading provides an opportunity to discuss relevant principles.
  • The bill is then submitted to the committee where it is considered clause by clause.
  • Most bills are sent to the standing committee where each committee deals with bills that belong to a specific range of topics and the committee reflects the strength of each party and has a representatives in its composition.
  • After reviewing the bill, the commission reports to the House of Representatives and amendments are proposed during further discussion. The bill is read and voted.
  • In addition to the bills proposed by the government, a limited number of bills sponsored by individual members will be considered by the House of Representatives in each session.
  • Since 1999, authority over many issues such as health, education, housing, transportation, the environment and agriculture has been transferred from the British Parliament to the newly established Scottish Parliament. the Wales National Parliament to Northern Ireland Irish Parliament.
  • The reallocation of legislative responsibility raised the question of whether parliamentarians in Scotland wreand should continue to vote for measures directed exclusively to England.
  • A new stage is standard in which legislation determined to affect only the kingdom is reviewed and voted by members of the United Kingdom before consideration by the House of Commons.
  • Similarly, legislation that only covered England and Wales was initially to be addressed only by British and Welsh lawmakers.
  • If it is unclear whether the measure is an England-only issue, the House of Representatives chairman was tasked with doing that.
  • Aside from passing the bill, full house’s most important business is the regular key questioning period.

What Is Parliamentary Sovereignty?

  • Now you’ll be thinking about that what is the concept of parliamentary sovereignty?
  • Parliamentary sovereignty means that parliament is superior to the executive and judicial branches of the government and can therefore enact or repeal any law it chooses.
  • It is a cornerstone of the UK constitutional system and also applies in some parts of the commonwealth such as Canada. So it is applied to UK as well as to Canada.
  • The idea of parliamentary sovereignty is neatly summed up by 19th century constitutional theorist A Dy.

Dicey’s Definition of Parliamentary Sovereignty

  • Parliament has under the English Constitution the right to make or unmake any law whatsoever.
  • And further, no person or body is recognized by the law of England as having the right to override or set aside the legislation of Parliament.
  • In theory, the UK Parliament could enact legislation which oppresses 95% of the population if it choose to do so.
  • However, the political consequences of such a measure means that in practice this is very unlikely to happen.
  • The notion of parliamentary sovereignty was at the heart of the Brexit referendum in 2016.
  • Those wishing to leave the European Union saw its institution such as the European Parliament as directly challenging the sovereignty of the Westminster Parliament because European laws can be automatically binding and override local laws within the UK.
  • On the other hand, it was Westminster Parliament which accepted some limits on its sovereignty when it decided to join the European Union in 1972 when it passed the European Communities Act.
  • Thus, it is likely that how that how the Parliament sovereignty is very unique.

A Principle of the British Constitution

  • It is very important to understand.
  • But then the parliamentary sovereignty is a principle of the British constitution.
  • This gives parliament the highest legal authority in the United Kingdom to create or terminate any law.
  • In general, courts cannot dismiss the law and Congress cannot pass legislation that future Congress cannot change.
  • Parliamentary sovereignty is the most important part of the British Constitution.
  • sovereignty can be defined for current purposes as having unlimited legal powers within a community.
  • But the term is used simply to mean the highest legal powers that is ahead of the state.
  • sovereignty also means national independence in international law.

Westminster’s Power to Pass, Amend or Repeal Laws

  • Parliamentary sovereignty means that the Westminster Parliament is legally entitled to pass, amend or abolish any law it desires.
  • If both the Senate and the House of Representative pass the bill and the monarch provisor ascent, courts and other agencies will not be able to revoke the law.
  • Parliamentary sovereignty is a statement of law, not a statement of politics.
  • Politically, Congress may not be able to pass any law.
  • The parliamentary monarch is sovereign or supreme in this sense and has the highest alternative legislative power in Britain. sovereignty raises some important issues. What is the basis of parliamentary sovereignty?
  • Can parliament really pass any law? and the current parliament is that if its successors will pass certain future laws, can you prevent it from being enacted? Parliamentary hegemony.
  • The doctrine of parliamentary hegemony is at the same time closely related to the medival idea of the rule of law by which all public powers including the king must obey the law at that time.

Bracton & the Distinction Between Referee & Ruler

  • There was no clear distinction between the public and the private.
  • In this book, Bracton makes a clear separation between the referee and the jurisdiction.
  • That is the realm of power or control is in the hands of the monarch and the realm of judicial administration to which the monarch was a part.
  • It was a feudal contract committing King John to respect the privileges of free men.

Parliamentary vs Judicial System in India

Video Part 2 · starts at 3:04:39

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • That is at the end of this session you will be able to understand the rule of law in India, the constitution of India, the judicial review and the Indian constitution and the concept of judicial activism.

Rule of Law in India

  • Now you might be wondering what is the concept of rule of law in India?
  • But before that understanding that what is rule of law in India, it becomes imperative to understand how does the parliamentary system in India actually works.
  • In India, the constitutional supremacy was explicitly reiterated in the Minerva Mill case whereby the Supreme Court held that the government, legislature, the executive and judiciary is all bound by the constitution and nobody is above or beyond the constitution.
  • Every law made by the parliament is subject to interpretation by supreme court in the light of ideals and objectives of the constitution and if they go beyond or above that they can be held null and void.
  • Indian constitution does not have express provision of separation of judicial and parliamentary supremacy but it’s not quite unclear as well.
  • It is a prerogative of the parliament to amend the constitution and make the laws.
  • It is a duty of the judiciary to decide the basic structure of the constitution are transgressed by such laws. One the parliament has done its job.

Judicial Review & Parliamentary Supremacy

  • It supreme court which decide its constitutionality through judicial review.
  • There have been conflicts between parliamentary supremacy and judicial supremacy.
  • The best example is of the National Judicial Appointments Commission where Supreme Court pronounced its verdict on the 189th constitutional amendment act and the National Judicial Appointments Commission declaring them to be ultra wires of the constitution.
  • It is true that constitution has given superior powers of review to judiciary to decide the constitutionality of the act passed by the legislature.
  • Distort of the judicial function should not be seen as against the will of the people.
  • Four, constitution derives its authority to give it this power to the judiciary.

Landmark Cases: Golaknath & Bank Nationalisation

  • The Supreme Court has enforced the power of judicial review in various cases.
  • For example, the Golaknath case in 1967, the bank nationalization case in 1970, the privy person’s abolition case in 1971, the Kesavananda Bharati case in 73, the Minerva Mills case in 1980 and the Supreme Court AO association versus union of India 2016.
  • Thus, the judiciary should be free from the influence of the executive so that it could promote the end of justice.
  • So this actually defines us that how the parliamentary and the judiciary with their own jurisdiction are still competitive enough in our country in India and how when the parliamentary supremacy is superior or judicial supremacy.

How the Rule of Law Is Followed

  • But it is always imperative to understand that how this is followed and how in turn the rule of law will be inserted with all of this.
  • Thus the rule of law in India is that in states where the rule of law is enforced there is a basic law or constitution from which all others law derive their power.
  • Its authority is limited because the monarch or national representative also complies with laws derived from the basic law.
  • The king or elected representative must act in accordance with the provisions of this law.
  • In the case of India, the constitution is the supreme law of land and all other laws and decree enacted by Indian government agencies must comply with the provisions of the constitution.
  • If it is not compliant, it will be declared invalid by the court.

Role of the Judiciary & Judicial Supremacy

  • Thus, the judiciary plays a very important role in maintaining the rule of law in India.
  • We have actually seen the judicial supremacy through various cases.
  • Further on, therefore, the constitutional principle of separation of powers is a breakwater against abuse of power and has a long lineage in the standard literature and constitutional theory.
  • The principle of separation of powers occupies a deep ambivalence in the whole picture of the principles that regulate constitutional governments in modern democracy around the world.
  • In line with the withdrawal of the constitutions of other nation states, our constitutional framework has also carved this popular principle of separation of powers in our constitution to check for abuse of power.
  • However, despite being a deeply pervasive feature of the constitution, the principle of separation of powers often is an area of fiction between different states.
  • The constitution of India is an organic document that draws its gravity in grand from the will of the Indians.

Separation of Powers: Philips & Wade

  • According to Phillips and Wade, doctrine of separation of powers can be defined as follows.
  • The same individual should not form more than one part of the government’s free institution.
  • One government organ should not exercise the function of another government organ.
  • One organ of government must not violate the functions of the other two organs of government.

Judicial Review in India

  • Under the provisions of the constitution of India, the legislature is entrusted with legislative power and the judiciary is responsible for interpreting such legislation and making decisions based on that interpretation.
  • Now you might be wondering what is a review on judicial review and the Indian constitution.
  • The constitution of India has created an independent judicial body empowered by judicial review to determine the legality of law and administrative measures.
  • Judicial review is an essential part to the rule of law.
  • Judicial review includes not only the constitutionality of the law but also the determination of the effectiveness of administrative measures.
  • All actions of state public authorities and bureaucrats subject to judicial review.
  • Therefore, they are all accountable to the court for the legality of their actions.

Procedure Established by Law & Judicial Activism

  • Judicial review in India follows the principle of procedure established by law.
  • Now, judicial activism is a further activity of judicial review.
  • Judicial activism is an essential aspect of the dynamics of the constitutional court.
  • However, judicial activity does not mean judicial rule.

Judiciary within the Constitutional Limits

  • It also needs to work within the judicial process.
  • Within those limits, it performs the function of justifying the actions of other government agencies or more rarely stigmatizing them.
  • Today, there’s a lack of harmony between the three state institutions, the state, header, the executive, and the judiciary.
  • It’s mainly because their powers are essentially overlapping.
  • Therefore, this conflict between the judiciary and the other two wings needs to be actively reconciled in order to achieve the constitutional vision and the implicit goal of separation of powers.

The Elected Parliament: Five-Year Term

  • Now, how does the elected parliament in Bangladesh work?

  • Formally, parliament enjoys an important position.

  • The validity of the parliamentary procedure cannot be questioned in any court.

  • In that case, the newly elected member must wait until the previous parliament’s term expires.

Electoral System & Constituencies

  • Bangladesh has traditionally followed the s simple single seat constituency electoral system.
  • In general, there is no significant deviation from the average of registered voters in single seat constituencies.
  • The franchisee is granted to everyone over the age of 18, but at least 25 people must run for parliamentary elections.
  • The candidate with the highest single vote is declared as elected.
  • Article 65 part one of the Bangladesh constitution stipulates that Bangladesh has a parliament in which the legislative power of the republic is granted in accordance with the provisions of this constitution.
  • However, Parliament may delegate the authority to create orders, rules, regulations, ordinances, and other documents with legislative effect to any person or authority.
  • The legislation has its roots mainly in the government.
  • Of the 1,152 bills enacted between 1973 and 2012, only seven were legislative bills. The rest were all government bills.

Features of Federalism

Federalism in India: Earlier Concepts

  • The central government started to become more overpowering over the state government and that was a clear indication that we are moving away from being purely federal.
  • So the famous political science philosopher KC has described this kind of federalism as cosy federalism where the central government becomes too dictatorship and becomes too overpowering on the state government and the state government starts to feel intervention.

Learning Outcomes for Federalism

  • This particular subtopic will be understanding about the features of federalism.
  • That is at the end of this session you will be able to define the concept of federalism.
  • You will also be able to describe the features of federalism.
  • The concept of federalism is quite dynamic and unique.

Division of Power Between Central & Regional Authorities

  • Federalism is a system of government in which power is divided between the central authority and the various constituent units of the country.
  • The commonwealth usually has two levels of government.
  • One is the government of the whole country which is usually responsible for some subjects of common national interest.
  • Others are state or state level governments that control much of the state’s day-to-day administration.
  • Both of these levels of government enjoy power independently of the other levels.
  • The central government can pass orders to state or local governments.
  • However, under the federal system, the central government cannot command the state government to do anything.
  • The state governments has its own authority in which central government cannot intervene.
  • Both of these governments are answerable to people separately.

Two or More Levels of Government

  • There are several features of federalism.
  • There are two or more levels or layers in a government.
  • Two different levels of government govern the same citizens, but each level has its own jurisdiction over specific legislative, taxable, and administrative issues.
  • The jurisdiction of each level or hierarchy of the three governments is specified in the constitution.
  • Therefore, the existence and authority of each layer of government is constitutionally guaranteed.
  • The basic provisions of the constitution cannot be unilaterally changed by a certain level of government.
  • Such changes require the consent of both levels of government.
  • Courts have the power to interpret the constitution and various levels of government authority.
  • The Supreme Court acts as a referee in the event of a dispute between different levels of government in exercising the respective powers.

Independent Sources of Income & Mutual Trust

  • The sources of income at each level of the government are clearly designated to ensure their financial autonomy.
  • Therefore, we can say the federal system has two purposes.
  • It is about protecting and promoting national unity and also about addressing regional diversity.
  • Hence these two aspects are important to the federalist system in practice.
  • Governments at different levels need to agree on some rules of power sharing.
  • They also need to trust that each one adheres to that part of the agreement.
  • The ideal federal system has both aspects of an agreement to live with mutual trust.
  • Federalism is a unique concept specifically in India with a parliamentary system of government.
  • But with the contemporary scenario and current trends, we have turned into a poly federal system.
  • Thus in the other few subtopics coming further we’ll be understanding the other consequence of federalism.

Federated States & Autonomy

  • Federalism is a numerous coordinating bodies distributing the power of the state among them where each is governed and controlled by the constitution.
  • Federated states have areas of autonomy that belong to territorial organizations called states.
  • As the constitution divides authority between central and local governments each drawing authority from the provisions of the constitution.
  • Both the federal and state governments draw power from the federal constitution both of which are best in a particular area and both work directly with the public.
  • Therefore, the state government is not exercising the power delegated by the federal government and is not subordinate to the federal government, although it may be dealing with less important issues.
  • Functional characteristics of the federal constitution. India has particularly a written constitution.
  • The federal state derives its existence from the constitution in the same way that a company derives it existence from the grants or legislation it was created.
  • All administrative, legislative and judicial powers, whether federal or member states are subordinate to and governed by the constitution.
  • Therefore, the federated states have written constitution for the obvious reasons that they must be accurately defined by written documents in order to be viable and stable and to enforce restrictions on them.

Dual Government

  • The second characteristics is dual government. The constitution establishes a dual government.
  • One government has authority over the country or the entire territory of the country that adopts its constitution that is the federal unit and the government of each region of which the federation is made up that is the federal unit.
  • The third characteristic feature of the federal constitution is a distribution of powers.
  • The most important feature of the federal system is a separation of powers between two government units, a country and a region.
  • However, even in a single system, there is distribution of authority and delegation of authority between the national and local governments.
  • But if the national government delegates its own authority to the local administration, the court cannot interfere.
  • The constitution distributes power between the two governments so that the respective government agencies of the two governments operate with direct authority over their citizens.
  • In the case of local governments, they have authority over citizens residing within the territory of the region and in the case of federal governments, its authority extends to citizens residing throughout the territory of the country. regardless of the territorial barriers of federal units.

No Unilateral Change

  • The fourth functional characteristic is no unilateral change.
  • The A4 mentioned separation of powers made by the constitution cannot be changed or amended by the unilateral will of the federal parties that is the federal or local government.
  • The constitution provides a process for changing its provisions called amendments.
  • The fifth function being interpretation by judiciary.
  • The separation of powers made by the constitution must be protected by a judiciary that interprets the constitution as a basic law of land.
  • Enforces it against both federal and local governments and invalidates any of those acts that transgress the restrictions imposed on them by the constitution.
  • All of these features are present on the one hand in the United States, Australian and West German constitutions and on the other hand in the Canadian and Indian constitutions.
  • Though the details may vary but the latter two are central and are strongly characterized by central bias or balance that supports the factors.
