Federalism vs Unitary System

Video Part 2 · starts at 3:58:23

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • That is at the end of this session you will be able to describe the features of unitary government and federal government.
  • You’ll also be able to describe the advantages and disadvantages of the unitary system of government and federal government.

What Is a Unitary System?

  • This is a very unique and dynamic subtopic where we will be contrasting the federal country of India and the unitary system which is mostly practiced in the United States of America. What is a unitary system?
  • A unitary government is a type of government system in which a single power called the central government controls the entire government.
  • In a unitary government, the central government has the power to increase or decrease the power of local governments. it can create the same and deprecate it.
  • The United Kingdom, Afghanistan, Italy, China, Saudi Arabia and Spain are important examples of a unitary government.
  • Centralization of the power and authority system continues to be a top priority.
  • As a unitary government system is based in the concepts of coherence, unity and identity.
  • People have a very limited voice in this government system.
  • So there aren’t many options for change and new innovation.

Features of Unitary Government

  • There are several features of the unitary government. First is a concentration of powers.
  • A unitary government is a government in which all administrative powers are granted to a single center.
  • A unitary state may be subdivided into smaller units for administrative convenience, but the units do not have their own constitutional status. Second point being single government.
  • A unitary government has a single government agency.
  • There is a single supreme legislature, a single executive branch and one supreme judiciary.

Written Constitution in Unitary Government

  • Another feature of unitary government is written on written constitution.
  • A unitary government may or may not enact a constitution.
  • For example, Great Britain and France are unitary states.
  • France has a constitution, but Britain has no constitution.
  • Rigid or flexible constitution unlike a federation, a unitary state may or may not have a rigid constitution.
  • That is the constitution of England is flexible but that of France is slightly rigid.

Judiciary in a Unitary System

  • They also do not have a special judiciary.
  • There is no need to have a special judiciary with wide range of veto powers in a unitary government.
  • For example, even the British Supreme Court cannot judge legislation passed by the parliament. The federal government.

Federal Government: Two Levels

  • The federal government is a type of national government in which the government has a part delegate authority to members of other elected states.
  • A country may have two levels of federal government running through a common body or through the powers set out in the state constitution.
  • In a federal government, a state or territory enjoys some of the rights available to an independent state.
  • However, international diplomacy, national security and other types of international transactions are carried out only by the federal government.
  • The hierarchy of power in the federal system begins at the federal level and flows to states and regions.
  • There are several features of the federal government.
  • In the federal government, administrative authority is divided between centers and units. Power may be distributed in two different ways.
  • The constitution states the authority that the federal authority should have and interests the rest of the federal unit.
  • Or the constitution states the authority that the federal unit should have and entrusts the rest to the federal authorities.

Federal Government: Separate Powers

  • Federal government also has a separate government.
  • In the form of federal government, both centers and units have separate sets of government agencies. America is a federation of states.
  • Therefore, the state has separate legislatures and separate executives. Federal government has a written constitution.
  • The federal government must write a constitution.
  • The federation is a political partnership in various states and requires a written agreement on the constitution. It also has a rigid constitution.
  • The composition of the federation must be more or less strict.
  • It is considered a sacred consensus and its spirit should not be easily violated.

Flexible Constitution & Special Judiciary

  • A flexible constitution allows a central government to reduce the autonomy of federal states. It also consists of a special judiciary.
  • In the commonwealth, there can be constitutional disputes between federal centers and units or between one unit and another.
  • All of these disputes are judged in the light of the constitution.

Advantages & Disadvantages of the Unitary System

  • There are several disadvantages and disadvantages of the unitary system.
  • The advantage of a unitary government is that it is a single and definitive legislation.
  • It is usually more efficient in using taxes, but fewer people try to get into the money.
  • Also the economy is easy to manage and the government is small.
  • The disadvantage of using this type of system is a slow government response.
  • For example, there are no national guards that could be dispatched in an emergency and troops would have to be mobilized by national authorities. It’s also easy to lose track of local issues.
  • Apart from that, it’s an incredibly disruptive form of government, forcing everyone to compete with everyone else for priority.
  • Finally, it has a huge bureaucracy that is even larger than what this country has.

Representation in a Federal System

  • Federalism provides representation to different groups.
  • Citizens of different states have different aspirations, ethnicities, and may follow different cultures.
  • The central government may overlook these differences and adopt policies that address the majority. Minority rights are also protected.
  • State governments have the freedom to adopt policies that the national body in other states cannot follow.
  • Sharing work between the central and local governments leads to optimal use of resources.
  • Federalism has room for innovation and experimentation.
  • The two local governments can have two different approaches to bring about reforms in all areas of the public domain. Be taxation or education. Disadvantages of federalism are many.

Costs & Overlaps in Power Sharing

  • Sharing power between the center and the state includes both the strengths and weaknesses of the federal government.
  • In some cases, the work can be duplicated and then confused as to who’s responsible for what.
  • The federal system is very expensive because more people elected than necessary in both the state and the central.
  • Thus, it is often said that only rich countries can afford it.

Unnecessary Competition & Regional Rebellion

  • It leads to unnecessary competition between different regions.
  • There could be a rebellion by the local government against the national government. It also promotes regional inequality.
  • Natural resources, industries, and employment opportunities vary by region. Finally, it does not eliminate poverty.
  • You’ve also learned and understood about the advantages and disadvantages of the unitary system of government and federal government.
  • In a few further subtopics, you’ll also be understanding about the various few constituent features of federalism.

Federalism – Historical Context of the USA

Video Part 2 · starts at 4:09:13

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • That is at the end of this session you will be able to define the concept of federalism.

American Federalism: An Innovative Experiment

  • It is a very unique and dynamic aspect in the concept of federalism in the course in comparative government and politics. What is federalism?
  • Federalism is one of the most important and innovative concepts in the US constitution.
  • But the term never appears in the country’s constitution.
  • Federalism is a sharing of power between national and state governments.
  • In the United States, states first existed and they struggled to create a national government. There’s a huge constitutional revolution.

A Stable Constitution

  • The constitution remains essentially intact over time.
  • The basic structure of government power is almost the same in the 21st as in late 18th century.
  • At the same time, the constitution has changed over the century since 1787.
  • The amendments have greatly expanded the freedom and rights of citizens.
  • The interpretation of the language by all three branches of the government brought the constitution into a territory that the founders have never imagined.
  • New practices have been ported to the ancient procedures of the constitution and intermediaries that were not listed in the constitution have in present times developed important government roles.

The 1787 Convention: Federation vs Unitary Rule

  • Part of the discussion at the 1787 constitutional convention focused on the basic government structure when proclaiming independence in 1776.
  • A highly centralized unitary government under the king was clearly rejected.
  • In contrast, the first US constitution, the articles of confederation promoted state coalitions with highly decentralized power, concentrated at the state level and weak central government.
  • The article was unsuccessful and the 1787 constitutional treaty was convened to change a government structure.
  • Rather than tweaking the structure of the condition, a third option was essentially invented.

Federal Design & Power Among Levels

  • This is today called the federal system of federalism.
  • Federal design provides power among multiple levels of government.
  • As defined, federalism is an institutional arrangement that creates a relatively autonomous level of government, each of which can act directly on behalf of the authorized people.
  • Further, under the federal system, authority is divided between national and state governments and the authority to act comes directly from the people.
  • Under a unitary system, the authority to act is concentrated solely on the government.
  • The powers delegated to lower level governments are exercised at the discretion of the state.
  • At the time of the 1787 convention, there were examples of both unitary government and confederations.
  • However, there is no example of inter intermediate option currently called the federal system. There are three types of government structure.
  • Within today’s government system, there are examples of all three types of government structures. Unitary, federal, and confederate structures.

Dual Federalism (1789–1945)

  • There’s also a unique definition of dual federalism from 1789 to 1945.
  • Dual federalism describes the nature of federalism in the world in the first 150 years of the United States from approximately 1789 to World War II.
  • The Constitution outlines the provisions of two types of government, national and state.
  • In the United States, in most cases, the national government dealt with defense, foreign policy, and the promotion of commerce.
  • While the state dealt with local issues, economic regulation and criminal law.
  • This type of federalism like lay cake is also called layer cake federalism because state and national governments have their own responsibilities and the different levels rarely overlap.

The 13 States & E Pluribus Unum

  • There’s also something called as eurous unam means one of many states.
  • In 1776, the newly independent state acted like 13 fighting entities.
  • These unified states had significant differences in history, geography, population, economy, and politics.
  • Each state wanted all the power of a sovereign state, signing treaties, receiving ambassadors, making money, and regulating commerce.
  • But they had to give up some of their powers to survive on the world stage.
  • To that end, they agreed to the Articles of Confederation, the first constitution of the United States.

Post-War Tensions & Industrialisation

  • After the end of the war in 1783, Union tensions regained and the country was in the verge of collapse.
  • There’s also been industrialization and globalization phase from 1865 to 1945.
  • The nature of US government and politics changed dramatically from the late 19th century to the early 20th century.
  • As a result of two major events, the country took on a greater role. These were industrialization.

A National Industrial Economy

  • The economy became a national industrial economy and the federal government had far better facilities than states to cope with this change. The second being globalization.
  • Due to its vast economy and its extensive trading network, the United States emerged as a global economic power.
  • The federal government since then has taken on a larger economic role as American companies and states have begun trading in large numbers abroad.
  • These events took place over decades but reached their highest points during President Franklin Roosevelt 1933 to 1945.
  • The current scenario being the US Constitution is sticking to the tensions of the struggle and Americans are still discussing the proper role of states versus a national government.
  • Federalism is a system of government in which the same territory is controlled by two levels of government.
  • In general, inclusive national governments are responsible for broader governance of larger territories, while smaller subdivisions such as states and cities given issues of regional concern.
  • Both the national government and smaller political subdivisions have the power to enact legislation and both have a certain level of autonomy from each other. There’s also a concept of bicalism.
  • The government is further complicated by dividing the parliament into two separate parliaments.

A Bicameral Legislature

  • The house of representatives and the senate.
  • Such a bicameral system was common in the state legislature.
  • The constitution equalizes the powers of the two parliamentary chambers and incorporates checks and balances within the legislature itself.
  • The bicameral system reminds us of the founders doubts about the majority vote.
  • They created the Senate to check the House of Representatives which was directly elected by the public.
  • With a six-year term in state legislature elections, senators were expected to work slowly to better understand the issue and manage the popular passions.

Recap: Dual Federalism in the USA

Why Centre–State Balance Matters

  • Why?
  • Because center becomes too overpowering on the state and lowers its jurisdiction.
  • Thus in United States they follow dual federalism that is both center and states are mostly autonomous bodies.
  • Dual federalism refers to the US government system which is 50 state governments and one federal government.
  • Some powers are delegated to the federal government and others remain in the state. Coming to the concept of hierarchy of laws.
  • Article Six of the United States Constitutional Amendment has long been interpreted to mean that federal law takes precedence over state law whenever the two conflict.
  • Conversely, federal power will was believed to be constrained by the first amendment, that is the bill of rights.
  • Many political scientists argued that dual federalism is no longer an exact term and state federal governments share power in a model that can be more accurately described as collaborative federalism.
  • How does a hierarchy of codes look like in the United States of America?

The Dual Court System

  • As a direct result of American federalism, there is a dual court system in the United States today.
  • There is a complete and independent federal court system and every state has a complete and somewhat independent state court system.
  • There is a complete and independent federal court system and every state has a complete and somewhat independent state court system.
  • Right beside you can see an image of the court system.

Court Hierarchy: Supreme Court & District Courts

  • At the apex level is a supreme court.
  • Next are the high courts, the district and session courts, the court of civil judge senior division, the civil judge s junior division and the moons courts and small causes courts. Now in India, how is it different?
  • In India, we only have Supreme Court, we have high court and we have district and revenue boards.
  • This is the distinction that we follow in the court system of India.

Congress & the Federal Judiciary

  • Particularly the Congress in United States decides how many judges should be and where should they work.
  • Congress is responsible for deciding which presidential candidate will eventually become a federal judge through the confirmation process.
  • Congress also approves federal court budgets and allocates funds to run the judiciary. The United States District Courts.
  • The US District Court is a first instance court of the federal court system within the limits set by Constitution and the Congress.
  • District courts have jurisdiction over hearing almost all categories of federal cases including both civil and criminal matters. The United States Court of Appeal.
  • The 94 US jurisdictions are organized into 12 regional circuits, each of which has a US Court of Appeals.
  • The Court of Appeal hears appeals from the district courts within its circuit and from federal agency decisions.
  • Since these codes are organized into circuit codes, they sometimes called circuit codes as well.

The United States Supreme Court

  • The United States Supreme Court, the USSC, The United States Supreme Court consist of the Chief Justice of the United States Supreme Court and eight associate judges.
  • At its discretion and within the specific guidelines established by Congress, the Supreme Court hears a limited number of cases for which a decision is required each year. The state code structures.
  • Each state’s constitution and law establishes state courts.
  • The last resort court, well known as the Supreme Court, is usually the best court in the state.
  • Some states also have an interim court of appeal.
  • Below these codes of appeal is a state court of first instance.
  • Some are called circuit courts or district courts.
  • Historically, states usually had codes dealing with specific legal issues like for example probate courts, juvenile courts and family courts.

Hierarchy of Laws: The Supremacy Clause

Bargaining & Negotiation Between Governments

  • However, cooperation was gradually redefined as state and local governments became more motivated to work with the federal government, especially in the 1960s.

Negotiation Among Federal, State & Local Officials

Political & Legal Foundations of Russia’s Federal System

🎯 Learning Objectives

Russia’s Constitutional Foundations

  • It is a quite interesting subtopic because in the few earlier subtopics we’ve read more about the concept of federalism in India and also particularly in the United States of America.
  • But this particular subtopic deals with the political and legal foundation of Russia’s federal system. Now let’s see what it circumscribes firstly.
  • Now let’s see what are the political legal foundations.

Separation of Powers in the Russian Constitution

  • The constitution of the Russian Federation establishes the principle separation powers.
  • Article 10 of the constitution stipulates that Russia’s state power is exercised on the basis of separation into independent legislative, executive and judicial departments.
  • State power is exercised by the president, the federal council consisting of the lower house and federal council and the governments and the courts.
  • The president of the Russian Federation is a head of the state.
  • He or she is the guarantor of the constitution and human rights of the Russian Federation. and the rights and freedom of the citizens and he or she adopts measures to protect the sovereignty of the Russian Federation, its independence and the integrity of the state, coordinated functioning in state government interaction.
  • He or she also determines the basic objectives of national and foreign policy and represents the Russian Federation in domestic and international affairs and is also the supreme commander the Russian armed forces.
  • Just like in the Indian case, the president is considered to be the supreme commander of the armed forces.
  • Russian citizens over the age of 35 who have permanently resided in the Russian Federation for more than 10 years may be elected as a president.

The President & Federal Districts

  • The president may be impeached by the Commonwealth House only if the basis of treason or another serious offense brought by the state Duma. There are various federal districts.
  • In 2000, a federal district is established in which the president appoints his or her planetary ambassador to increase the effectiveness of the activities of federal agencies and improve the system governing the execution of their decisions.
  • Federal district is not a region or another constitutional part of the administrative division of the Russian Federation.

Executive Power & the Prime Minister

  • The executive power particularly that is the administrative power is exercised by the government of the Russian Federation.
  • The chair, the prime minister is appointed by the president with the consent of the state Duma.
  • The government is made up of the chairman, vicechairman and federal minister of the government of the Russian Federation.
  • Governments lead systems of federal government agencies such as ministries, federal services and federal agencies.
  • How does a local government look like in Russia?
  • The local autonomy provides the independent solution of important local problems by the population as well as the ownership, use and management of local government property.
  • It is exercised by citizens through referendums, elections and other forms of direct statement of intention and through other electoral local government agencies.

The Russian Legal System

  • How does legal system in Russian Federation work? The Russian Federation has a civil law system.
  • Russian law includes both codified laws, for example, civil and criminal law and other laws that in principle must be consistent with the relevant law.
  • Legislation has a highest jurisdiction compared to the articles of association and other sources of law.
  • However, the constitutional court can invalidate unconstitutional laws as well.
  • International law is recognized as part of the Russian legal system.
  • Case law can be recognized as a de facto source of law and the Supreme Court’s ruling is also binding a lower courts to ensure uniformity of legal practice.
  • Now the legal systems in the Russian Federation work in an integrative manner. Now what else?
  • Let’s see how the Russian Federation works in consonance and how the parliament endorses international treaties on human rights, the Russian territorial integrity and the principles of international affairs and the Russia’s participation in international organization.

Court Structure & Hierarchy

  • The court structure and hierarchy of Russia.
  • The Russian court structure consists of a constitutional court, a federal court and a district court, both of which are headed by the Supreme Court.
  • Federal courts include the commercial court that settle economic disputes and the general court that handle all other cases.
  • District Court include, the magistrate’s court which resolves minor issues and the constitutional statutory courts of the Russian region which handle cases relating to regional constitution decree.
  • The constitutional court deals with disputes over the constitutionality of certain legal acts and is a separate judiciary in the Russian court system.
  • Courts of general jurisdiction are magistrate courts and district courts.

Courts of Cassation & the Supreme Court

  • Courts of regions that is the appalate court of the regions cassation and the supreme court and commercial courts are the commercial courts of the regions.
  • The commercial affinate courts, the district commercial court, casigation and supreme court. There are various types of court. First being the supreme court in Russia. The supreme court is a court of cassation.

Courts of Appeal & Oversight

  • Second trial.
  • The oversight court that handles cases relating to the Supreme Court’s judicial opposition and makes final decision in both the general and the commercial court.
  • The courts in all other public institutions are legally bound by resolutions of the constitutional court.
  • The precedium decision or the Supreme Court’s main meeting constitutes the basis for amending the lower court’s decision provided the decision includes a direct reference to its retroactive effect.
  • In similar cases, specialized court such as the intellectual property court, which is a specialized commercial court that resolves dispute relating only to intellectual property rights.
  • There’s also military court which form a part of a general court system that handles all cases involving military officers and a commercial court which handle cases related to economic activity, bankruptcy and corporate issues.
  • There are also federal level courts such as the human rights ombudsman, children rights ombudsman, the business ombudsman, the regional ombudsman.
  • Alternate dispute resolution including mediation and arbitration is becoming more popular in Russia due to reasons like increasing case load of state courts.
  • The time involved in resolving the dispute with the courts etc.
  • Judicial independence is one of the main constitutional principles of the Russian legal system and judges abound only by constitutional and federal law.

  • Urban settlements are not necessarily different from rural settlement in terms of the regional populations.
  • The label differences are based in socioeconomic characteristics that make urban settlements more economically independent as compared to rural settlements.
  • Since 2000, there’s also federal government at the territorial level.

  • The territorial structure of the Russian Federation has undergone some changes in recent decades.
  • The federation encourages a subject to merge as many of them are no longer economically viable.

Local Budgets & Federal Law 131

Unfunded Mandates & Local Finance

  • Nevertheless, unfunded missions continue to be one of the major challenges of Russia’s local government.

  • The budget code defines the tax authority of the region and determines the sharing of taxes between different levels of government and leveling schemes.

Unitary vs Federal States

  • In the few earlier subtopics we read and understood about how the concept of federalism came into perspective and how it is a very unique and dynamic concept.
  • But the term federalism is used in countries such as Russia which is considered to be a multi-level federation, United States of America which is considered to be dual federalism and India which became a federal country when it received its independence but in practicality it turned into federal which was particularly coined by the unique and dynamic western philosopher called as Casey wear who said that when the center in India turns to be overpowering over the state and starts to intervene we no longer are a federation we turn into quasi federal that is half federal rather In this subtopic particularly we going to learn and understand about the concept of unitary government.
  • You might be wondering what is particularly the concept of unitary government.
  • A single government system or unitary state is a sovereign governed as a single entity.
  • The administrative divisions exercise only the powers delegated by the central government.
  • Subdivision units have been created and deprecated and their authority may be expanded or reduced by the central government.
  • Some media political philosophers defined a single form of government as follows.
  • According to Garner, the place where the full power of the government is given to a single central body by the constitution is referred to as a unitary government.
  • According to CF Strong, there are two important qualities of a single government.

The United Kingdom as a Unitary State

  • The example of unitary state is the United Kingdom which is made up of four countries England, Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.
  • Although technically a constitutional monarchy, Britain functions as unitary state and the parliament, the legislature of the country is in London, England has total political power.
  • Each other country in the United Kingdom has its own government, but it cannot enact laws that affect other parts of the United Kingdom or refuse to enforce legislation enacted by the parliament.
  • Another example of unitary government is France.
  • In the Republic of France, the central government has full control over about thousand local administrative divisions of the country called sectors.
  • Each department is headed by the chief executive officer appointed by the French central government.
  • Although they are technically governments, the French regional sector exist only to implement the directives issued by the central government.

Other Unitary States: Italy, Japan, China & the Philippines

  • Other notable unitary states include Italy, Japan, the People’s Republic of China, and the Philippines.
  • Now, we further move on to explaining unitary states versus federations. The opposite of a unitary state is federation.
  • A federation is constitutionally organized union or alliance of a partially autonomous state or other region under central federal government.
  • Unlike local governments where most of unitary state is powerless, federal states enjoy some degree of independence in their internal affairs.

Federalism in the United States

  • The structure of the US government is a good example of the Commonwealth.
  • The US Constitution establishes a system of federalism in which power is shared between the central government of Washington and the governments of 50 individual states.
  • Even in the Commonwealth, the distribution of power is often a source of controversy.
  • In the United States, for example, a dispute over state rights, the division of constitutional power between the federal and state governments is a common subjective decisions issued by the US Supreme Court under its original jurisdiction.
  • There are also unitary and authoritarian states which are quite opposite to a unitary state.
  • In an authoritarian state, all governing and political power is conceived on a single individual leader or a small elite group of individuals.
  • The leaders of authoritarian nations are neither chosen by the people nor constitutionally liable to them.
  • Authoritarian states rarely allow freedom of speech, press or practice religions not approved by the state.
  • In addition, there are no provisions to protect minority rights.
  • Nazi Germany under Adolf Hitler’s administration is commonly cited as a typical authoritarian state.

Authoritarian Unitary States & Advantages

  • Modern examples include Cuba, North Korea and Iran.
  • Now there are certain advantages of a unitary state. Firstly, there is quick response.
  • Decisions are made by a single governing body, allowing a single government to respond more quickly to unforeseen circumstances, whether domestic or foreign.
  • Cost can be easily reduced without a common federal multi-level government.
  • Bureaucracy, a unitary state can operate more efficiently. potentially reducing the tax burden on its population. It can be also smaller.
  • The unitary state can govern the entire country from a single place with a minimum number of or elected officials.
  • The small structure of unitary state allows it to meet the needs of people without the need for a large workforce.
  • There are certain disadvantages of a unitary state.

Disadvantages of Unitary States

  • Firstly, insufficient infrastructure.
  • Even if decisions can be made quickly and government may lack the physical infrastructure needed to implement the decisions in natural emergencies such as natural disasters. Lack of infrastructure can endanger people. Local needs can be ignored.
  • Single governments tend to focus in diplomacy while differing domestic needs as it can take time to develop the resources needed to respond to the circumstances that arise.

Risks: Abuse of Power

  • It also promotes abuse of power.
  • In a single state, one person or legislative body holds most if not all government power.
  • History shows that it can easily be abused if it is placed in hand that is too weak.
  • You also read about the unitary states versus federations and unitary states versus authoritarian states.
  • The advantages and disadvantages of a unitary state.
  • In the next sub few subtopics, we’re going to read and understand more about the constituents of unitary government.

Features of Unitary Government

  • In the earlier subtopic we read and understood about what is unitary government.
  • In this subtopic we’ll be understanding about the main features of unitary government. Now what is a unitary government?
  • A single government is a type of government system in which power called the central government controls the entire government.
  • In a single government, the central government has a power to increase or decrease the power of local governments. It can create the same and deprecate it.
  • Centralization of the power and authority system continues to be a top priority.
  • As a single government system is based on the concepts of coherence, unity and identity.

Limited Voice of the People

  • Checking the software with any one value is as good as checking with all other values of that equivalent class.
  • People have a very limited voice in this government system.
  • So there aren’t many options for change and new innovation. The basic features of unitary government.

Centralisation of Power

  • The first one being centralization of power.
  • In a single system all power is centralized in the hands of central government.
  • The center of which is a reservoir of power in all states.
  • In this system, there is no state government and the constitution empowers the central government to legislate, enforce and arbitrate with all its might. There’s also single and simple government. A single government is a very simple system.
  • With the exception of the United Kingdom, there is no state legislature, executives or senate in the center. There is one central government in the center.

Unicameral Legislature & Low Cost

  • There is a unicameal legislature that is generally elected.
  • Their cost are low and the states operate under a unified command.
  • It’s a simple and easy to understand system and general public easily understands its structure and power. The third feature being legal unity.

Law Enacted in One Unified System

  • Another feature of a single government is that unlike federal government, the law is enacted only by a single central government throughout the state. So the law of a single system is unified.
  • The legislation created by the center will be enforced in the rest of the state as well.
  • But in the commonwealth, the nature of the law varies from state to state.
  • The third feature being no separation of powers.
  • The federal state constitution distributes power between the central and the states.
  • But in a single system there is no list of separation of powers in the constitution. All power belongs to the central government.
  • In this system, the government pays entire attention to development as it is free from this power distribution business. Another feature being flexible constitution.
  • The constitution of a unitary state is inherently flexible.
  • Only federations require a strict constitution to establish a strong and secure relationship between the center and the federation unit.

Flexibility & the Possibility of Tyranny

  • The advantage of a single system configuration is that it can be modified in response to time needs and changing circumstances. Next, pink tyranny possibility.
  • One of the key features of this system is a potential for tyranny.
  • If the ruler is loyal and patriotic, all privileges are under the control of the center and there are no checks on the exercise of these unlimited privileges.
  • The lack of an internal checking system makes it absolute and can abuse state power responsibility.
  • This system is definitely more responsible than the federation.
  • Fixed responsibility in a defined institution is an important feature of this form of state.
  • The legislative assembly is responsible for legislation.
  • The executive branch responsible for implementation and the judiciary is responsible for arbitrrage.
  • These institutions are responsible for their constitutional responsibilities and are doing their best to stay within the limits of national law.

  • It provides a better range for bringing unity among people.

Speed of Decision & Firmness of Action

  • In a single government, people have to rely solely on the central government for everything.

  • However, this limits the control that the central government can have over its smaller units.

Advantages & Examples

  • Compared to the federal system, a single system is considered more powerful.

  • Unified legislation ensures that there is no tension as a public is less likely to be upset by legislation that applies nationwide.

Separation of Powers Doctrine in the United Kingdom

Video Part 2 · starts at 5:55:07

🎯 Learning Objectives

Separation of Powers in the UK

  • In the earlier few subtopics we read and understood about how unitary government its constituents features and functions and how’s unitary government different from federal government.
  • What is the separation of powers doctrine in the United Kingdom?
  • The main elements of this theory are government agencies fall into three categories. Legislature, government and judiciary. Government has three specific functions.
  • Functions need to be separated to ensure freedom.
  • The three branches of government shall consist of completely separate and distinct groups of people with no overlapping membership.
  • Each branch of government acts as a check for the arbitrary exercise of authority by other branches.

John Locke & the Legislative Power

  • John Locke’s analysis showed that legislative power was the highest distinguishing between administrative and federal rights. one related to the enforcement of domestic law in the state and the other to state security and external relations.
  • But still he said they are almost always in the hands of the same person.
  • In other words, the community interest political power to the government.
  • Therefore, Loach’s analysis strictly speaking does not explain the doctrine of separation of powers.
  • The doctrine was fully extended by the hands of Charles Louis de Secondat also known as Baron de Montesquieu.

Locke on Tyranny & the Separation of Powers

  • He felt that the history of the tyrannical tudtor in the absolute taught showed that freedom was not secured if administrative and legislative powers were held in the same hand.
  • He inferred the idea of separation of powers from his observations and the idea of the relationship between kings Stewart and Parliament.
  • He believed that Congress was by no means arbitrary and that denial of legislative power over the king could make improvised decree rule impossible.
  • Maitlin dates the doctrine of separation of powers in England to the reign of Edward one.
  • He observes that all three elements existed in the form of parliament, the royal council and the courtroom.
  • Count Henry St. John Bolingbroke developed the idea of separation of powers in his book opinions on the history of England.
  • He focused in the balance of power within the constitution as imbalances destroy the constitution.

Balance Between King, Parliament & People

  • He argues that a balance between the king, parliament and the people is needed to protect the freedom and security of the nation.
  • The United Kingdom has some sort of separation of powers, but unlike the United States, it is informal.
  • The mixed government Blackstone theory of check and balance is more relevant to Britain.
  • Separation of powers is not an absolute dominant feature of the British Constitution.
  • The three branches are not formally separated and continue to overlap significantly.
  • The United Kingdom is increasingly interested in the separation of powers especially with article six of the European Convention on Human Rights which protects the right to a fair trade.

Constitutional Reform Act 2005 & the Supreme Court

  • The Constitutional Reform Act of 2005 reforms the position of Lord High Chancellor and the Pope ceizes to enter the legislature.
  • Article 23 of the act provides for the establishment of the Supreme Court of the United Kingdom.
  • The Supreme Court which has been separated from parliamentary powers has been in operation since October 2009.
  • Article 61 of the 2005 constitutional amendment act provides for the constitution of the judicial appointment committee for the appointment of judges in the supreme court and courts.
  • Therefore most of the independence of the judiciary has been ensured by the constitutional reform act 2005. The separation of powers in England.
  • The British constitution is not based in the separation of powers.
  • The British idea of mixed governance is based in the belief that the degree of connection not separation provides a check and balance of the government system. The crown is a nominal head of England.

Fusion of Powers in the Cabinet

  • It is a cabinet of prime ministers who are true secretary generals.
  • Therefore, a single organization exercises legislative and administrative functions.
  • It also includes civil servants who run the administration of the country and the armed forces and police who defend the executive branch.
  • The role of the government is to formulate and implement government policies.
  • An elected government is responsible for the parliament.
  • The parliament also consists of a king, an elected house of commons and an unelected house of commons.
  • The House of Commons is superior to the House of Commons in legal enactment.

An Uncodified Constitution

  • The British Constitution is an uncodified constitution.
  • Parliamentary sovereignty is a basic principle of the British Constitution. The judiciary includes all judges in court.
  • The appointment of the higher judiciary is made by the king.
  • One of the unique features of the British legal system is a system of the Lord Chancellor who is a head of the judiciary responsible for the appointment of other judges.
  • He is also a member of parliament and plays an important role in the government.
  • Lord Chancellor also presides over the House of Lords as its speaker.
  • This role of the Lord High Chancellor is often criticized for violating the separation of powers and for concentrating administrative, legislative and judicial functions of one person.

Judicial Interpretation & the Doctrine

  • In situations where controversial political and social issues are subject to the scope of the judiciary, the court alleges a lack of jurisdiction based in this doctrine of separation of powers to avoid conflict between the judiciary and the political system.
  • Especially in the case of legal interpretation, they use the words of separation of powers to explain and justify their decisions.
  • Therefore, in the United Kingdom scenario, the doctrine is not clearly recognized in the constitutional plan, but it is not in absolute form.

Legislative Bodies in China

  • Various types of administrative decisions from China’s laws, regulations, and national and local government levels affect almost every aspect concerning China.
  • Therefore, it is important to understand the process by which these laws and regulations are created.

Legislative System & Development of Chinese Legislation

  • Firstly, the China’s current legislation structure includes the legislative system and the legislation structure.
  • The development of contemporary Chinese legislation includes a new turning point.
  • The legislation of the NPC and its standing committee includes the legislative powers of the NPC.
  • The legislative procedures of the NPC legislation of the NPC standing committee NPC standing committee’s legislative competence legislative procedures of the NPC standing committee and a better legislative institution.

Regulations by the State Council

  • Creation of regulations by the state council and its departments.
  • The legislative function of the state council, the scope of regulation making by the state council, the completion of the legislation of the state council and the regulation making by departments of the state council.
  • There’s also what we call as a local legislation in China.
  • There are basic principles of local legislation.
  • General local legislative power, local legislation in ethnic autonomous areas and legislation in special economic zones and special administrative regions.
  • There are certain central legislative bodies in China.
  • The National People’s Congress, the NPC, is the highest legislative body of the Chinese government.

The National People’s Congress & Its Standing Committee

  • The full NPC consists of approximately 3,000 members.
  • Each member being selected by the states people congress or the military and meeting for only two weeks each march.
  • Due to the infrequent frequency of these meetings, NPCs need to rely in various committees to carry out the normal task of drafting central government legislation.
  • The NPC standing committee is the most important NPC committee and consists of approximately 150 members which meet every two months.
  • The standing committee chairman’s council in which the NPC chair and vice chair sit with the chairs of the nine permanent NPC committees plays an important role in legislative activity.
  • The State Council is the country’s highest executive branch and is often referred to by the western media as a Chinese cabinet.
  • It consists of the prime minister, the deputy prime minister, the state council members, the audit president, the general secretary, numerous ministers and members from various state agencies directly under the legislature.
  • The legislative council of the state legislature, LAO, sets the legislative agenda of the legislature and where applicable coordinates the implementation of legislation by the state agencies.

The Legislative Affairs Office

  • In total, 70 to 80% of the laws promulgated by the NPCs go directly from or through LAO.
  • The office also enforces and evaluates the law by the responsible agency.
  • Coordinates between ministries enforces the law. Central and local laws and regulations.
  • Overseas registration and issuance of laws and accreditations registration of central and local laws and rules and the publication of legislation in authorized translations.

Administrative Levels & Autonomous Regions

  • The lower administrative level, the national level of government agencies in the state level includes four municipalities that is Beijing, Chongqing, Shanghai and Tianjin.
  • It also includes five autonomous regions, the Guangxi, the Inner Mongolia, Ningxia, Tibet and Xinjiang.
  • There are certain types of legislation which includes the national law.
  • These are issued by NPCs in their standing committees.
  • The national administrative regulations are prepared and issued by the legislature and its various agencies in accordance with the constitution and the law.

Administrative Rules

  • Under the leadership of the state council, central government agencies may develop administrative rules in specific policy areas as long as these rules do not conflict with the constitution, existing laws, and the state council’s own administrative rules and regulations.
  • If no previous decisions have been made on a particular subject, the regulatory measures of the ministries and committees can serve the function of law.
  • The administrative regulation of a country has a great influence because of its general scope and its use as a legal basis for decisions at the lower level of government. There also regional regulations.
  • These are issued by the state and lower level people’s assembly.
  • These regulations cannot be inconsistent with constitutional or higher government laws and administrative regulations.
  • The standing committee of the upper people’s assembly has a right to invalidate the decision of the lower people’s assembly. Regional and local administrative rules.
  • These are issued by the state and lower level people’s governments and must be promulgated by all higher governments and subject to the decisions of their national parliament.

Agenda Setting & Drafting

  • The legislative processes includes agenda setting.
  • In general, most legislation goes through a basic agenda setting process. The topic will be investigated. Legislation will be drafted.

Proposals from Ministries & Top Leaders

  • Most proposals come from ministries and their leaders.
  • Ministry officials statements at the request of top leader advisors conduct surveys and feasibility studies. collect and analyze the results of regional policy experiments.
  • They use this background check to draft proposals and in some cases bills. Writing and drafting.
  • Ministries are usually responsible for drafting legislation.
  • The LAO receives a draft, investigates it, makes the necessary adjustments and then sends it to the legislature or NPC. In some cases, the law is written by LC staff.
  • Drafting agencies often seek the opinions of experts, scholars, implementing agencies and other stakeholders in this area.

Executive System of China

Video Part 2 · starts at 6:15:18

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • That is at the end of this session, you will be able to define the executive system of China.
  • You will also be able to describe the various constituents attached to it.

Executive System of China

  • Understand about the executive system of China.
  • In the few earlier subtopics, we read and understood about the legislative system of China.
  • But in this subtopic particularly, we’re going to understand about the executive system of China.
  • You might be wondering how is the executive system of China different from the legislative process.
  • The Chinese government follows five power systems advocated by its late father and founder of the republic Sun Yat-sen.
  • This form of government combines the key characteristics of the cabinet and the presidential government.
  • The concept of the government’s five forms of power is clear from the parliament and its functions, the position of the president of five or more unan and the independence of the powers of examination and supervision.

The 1946 Constitution & the Cabinet

  • The constitution adopted by the national assembly in 1946 has provisions specific to the cabinet office.
  • This means that the prime minister must be the leader of most of the legislature or at least a person who supported independent by the majority.
  • The vice president of the executive yuan and the minister of nonprofit are appointed by the president and the recommendation of the prime minister and therefore show joint responsibility.
  • The prime minister must also co-sign the executive order.

The Executive Yuan & Legislative Responsibility

  • The executive yuan is responsible for the legislative yuan and the prime minister must resign if he fails to carry out legislative yuan resolution on his presidential objections.
  • As a result, the cabinet is directly responsible to the legislature.
  • However, the legislative yuan cannot pass a distrust resolution and resign from the executive yuan.
  • And the executive yuan has not been invested with the autonomy or with the authority to dissolve the legislative yuan.
  • On the other hand, two characteristics of the presidential system are recognized.
  • One is that the executive yuan with the approval of the president can send the bill back to the legislative yuan for redeliberations.
  • Second, the members of the legislative yuan cannot work in government at the same time.

A Unique Position Among Government Systems

  • Neither of these features is possible in a pure cabinet type government.
  • Therefore, China’s government system occupies a unique position in the world’s political system.
  • This is a product of Dr. Sun’s conception of five power constitution which lies between the cabinet system and the presidential system.
  • How does a national assembly of China look like?

Four Political Rights of the National Assembly

  • Theoretically, the National Assembly exercises four major political rights on behalf of the people. election, recalls, initiative and referendums.
  • The election of the president and the vice president.
  • It may bring back the president or vice president to amend the constitution.
  • No initiative or referendum power can be exercised at this time except in the case of the constitutional amendment submitted by the legislative yuan in a referendum.
  • It is similar to that of American electoral college in some respects as the president, the vice president are elected by the electoral college.
  • However, in many other respects, it has the same powers as Congress, especially in actions such as voting for constitutional amendments and changing existing borders.
  • Recently the great legal council ruled that in the interpretation of the constitution the parliament along with the legislative and control yuan is equivalent to the parliament of democracies.
  • The president and the vice president is elected by the national assembly for a six-year term and may be reelected for the second term.
  • He is selected from candidates proposed by more than 100 representatives of the national assembly.
  • The vice president is elected under the same rules as the president and has the same term of office.
  • The position of the vice president is somewhat like the corresponding position under the US constitution.
  • He takes over the presidency if the presidential palace becomes weakened until his former term expires and exercises a function of the presidency in case the president becomes incapacitated for some reason.

The Five Yuan: Examination & Supervision

  • The five yuan as Sun Yat-sen advocated in addition to the usual administrative legislative and judicial powers there must be review and supervision or administrative purpose.
  • The former refers to an open competition testing system for applicants for public vocational and technical professions.
  • The latter control yuan is a system for impeachment, civil servant supervision and auditing.
  • The five major branches of the government of the republic of China independent of each other.
  • The advantage of using a five power system is that Gosha presents the executive EAN from filling public post with incompetent personal or selecting personal according to personal preference.
  • In addition, the independent impeachment function would prevent legislative opposition parties from using impeachment as a political tool.

The Committee System

  • Then comes the committee system. There are two types of committee.
  • The standing committees and the special committees.
  • The standing committee consider bills and resolutions originally mentioned.
  • The special committees handle specific issues as credentials or maintainers rules and accounts.

The Legislative Yuan & the Budget

  • The legislative yuan exercises its legislative powers to determine legislation and confirm emergency legislation.
  • Some other work areas include it manages the budget. It has power over diplomacy.
  • It discusses and makes decisions on martial law and general assembly presented by the president and heads of various ministries of the executive yuan.
  • It also has a power to change government policy.
  • In this session thus you’ve learned about the executive system of China, the national assembly, the function of national assembly and the president and the vice president.

Judicial System of China

  • In the few earlier subtopics we read and understood about the legislative and the executive pro system in China particularly and the few earlier subtopics we read and understood about the unitary and federal forms of government. Now, what is the judicial system of China?
  • China’s judicial system is based on three-step court principles.
  • Within the framework of the judicial yuan, there are three different judicial bodies.
  • The Supreme Court, the administrative court, the committee in the decide in the discipline of public functionaries.

The Judicial Yuan

  • The judicial yuan is administratively superior to these three referees.
  • But under no circumstances does it prevent you from exercising your demoted judicial power.
  • It has a power to interpret constitutions and laws.
  • A large council of judges has been set up exclusively to take charge of matters concerning the interpretation of the constitution and the unified interpretation of the law. The judiciary function and authority.

Courts & Judicial Assistance

  • The courts attempt the case and handle the case of handover and judicial assistance.
  • Proerates to review an approve an application for arrest, start a prosecution, to start a public interest proceeding, to investigate certain types of crimes, oversee courts, public security agencies, detention centers, and prisons.
  • Supervisory Commissions investigate allegations of illegal activity related to the obligation of civil servants and impose administrative sanctions on them.
  • Investigate suspected crimes related to the duties of civil servants.
  • Transfer to the public prosecutor’s office and summit to the public prosecutor’s office.
  • The public security organs investigate crimes excluding crimes specifically investigated by supervisory board.
  • The public prosecutor’s office in the department of state security and if necessary transfer the case to the public prosecutor’s office for prosecution.
  • Take compulsory measures such as detention for criminal suspects. imposing public security sanctions on criminals for his her conduct that disrupts social order but is not sufficient to constitute a crime.

Judicial Administration & Supervisory Organs

  • The judicial administrative organs manages attorneys, legal assistants, foreign examinations, polarization and arbitration, organizes a nationally unified legal professional qualification exam and regulates prisons and drug addiction treatment centers. handle government related administrative reviews and litigation cases at the same level.
  • The relationship between judicial organ and people’s congress are the same level.
  • Chinese authorities are divided into five levels from central government to local government.
  • The central government, the government’s provincial levels, municipal levels, country levels, and township levels.
  • Governments at each level have a people’s congress, but judicial bodies are only established at the central, state, city, and country levels.
  • Each judiciary is under the supervision of the same level of people’s congress.
  • Throughout the election, the people’s congress appoints leaders at the same level of judiciary, the courts, proctorates and the supervisory commission.
  • The Supreme People’s Court oversees trials heard by local and specialized people’s courts at various levels.
  • The higher level people court oversees the trials heard by the lower people’s court.

The Supreme People’s Procuratorate

  • The proctor, the supreme people’s procure shall lead various levels of local and specialized prosecutors offices.
  • The higher level prosecutor shall lead the lower level prosecutor and the national supervisory board shall lead and direct the work of regulatory supervisory boards of all levels and the high level supervisory board shall lead the work of the lower supervisory board.
  • The judicial administrative organs shall be subject to the professional guidance of high level judicial organs and shall implement the rules and decisions promulgated by them.
  • However, the higher judiciary generally does not have the authority to revoke or change the decisions of the lower judiciary.

Jurisdiction of the Supreme Court

  • The Supreme Court exercises jurisdiction over the following matters. Appeal against a criminal case.
  • Decision by the high court at the first instance of the branch high court.
  • Appeals against judgments in both civil and criminal cases issued by the high court of the branch high court at the second instance court.
  • A motion to revoke a decision of the high court at the branch high court. The civil service discip discipline committee. It is jurisdiction over all civil servants.
  • The committee consists of a chairman of the specially appointed rank who is a manager of the entire organization and 9 to 15 members of the elected rank who are responsible for deciding matters concerning prince disciple.

The People’s Court System & Criminal Procedure

  • Strictly speaking China’s judicial system refers to people’s court system.
  • According to the code of criminal procedure of China, the people’s court, the people’s prosecutor’s office and the public security agency shall carry out the respective duties incorporated in the criminal procedure.
  • In this sense, people’s prosecutors and public security agencies both exercise jurisdiction.
  • The people’s court is a judicial body that exercises jurisdiction on behalf of the state.
  • According to the 1979 constitution revised in 1983 in the organic law of the people’s court, China practices a court system characterized by four levels in two court cases.
  • China’s jurisdiction is exercised by the following people’s court. local people’s court of various levels, military courts, other special people’s court and the supreme people’s court.
  • Thus, in the next few other subtopics, we’ll be learning more about the Chinese constituents and its parliamentary system.

Electoral Process in China

Electoral Process: Overview

  • In the few earlier subtopics we read about the party system in China where it started everything with a pure matang party in later ended with a communist party and till date it follows a system of communism and communist party being the popular in China.
  • But in this particular subtopic we will be reading an understanding about the electoral process in China and what are its various constituents. Now what is the electoral process in China?
  • The electoral system here refers to the way citizens choose state civil servants.
  • The election system of the people’s republic of China here refers to the election of members of the people’s assembly at various levels.
  • Parliamentary elections for the people’s assembly include general local elections between special administrative regions and Taiwanese come patriots and parliamentary elections from the military.
  • The general elections apply to the selection of local and ethnically autonomous region members.
  • China has five central and local level people’s conferences that is they are the national people’s assembly the people’s assembly of states autonomous regions and municipalities ities under the central government.
  • The people’s assembly of cities divided into district and the people’s assembly of cities not divided into autonomous regions, districts, municipalities and county. autonomous county and townships, ethnic minority townships and township meetings.
  • You might be wondering, how does a Chinese election system work?

Voting Rights & Local Elections

  • All citizens of the People’s Republic of China who have reached the age and have the right to vote regardless of the ethnic background, race, gender, occupation, family background, religious beliefs, education level, property, status or length. You have the right to run for election.
  • That means these people regardless of all these constituents at the right to run for the election system in China.
  • Those who have been deprived of their political rights in accordance with the law do not have the right to vote and do not have the right to run for election.
  • One voter can only vote one vote in each election. That is one vote has one value.
  • Members of the people’s assembly of districts, municipalities, counties, autonomous counties, townships, ethnic minority townships and cities that are not divided into towns are directly elected by their members.
  • The NPC members in states, autonomous regions, cities, towns and villages with the central government.
  • Cities divided into district and the people’s assembly of autonomous regions are elected by the following lower levels people’s assembly.

Nomination of Candidates

  • All political parties and mass organizations can nominate candidates to parliamentarians jointly or individually.
  • Candidates may also be nominated by a minimum of 10 voters in direct elections and 10 members in indirect elections.
  • In both direct and indirect elections, the number of candidates must exceed the number of delegates elected and elections must be competitive.
  • In direct elections, number of candidates must exceed one/ird to 100% of the number elected.
  • In indirect elections, the number of candidates must exceed 20% to 50% of the number elected.
  • Voters can vote for a candidate, vote against a candidate, vote for vote for someone else or abstain.
  • In direct elections, more than half the voters in the constituency must vote for the election to take effect and the candidate who receives the majority of the vot cast is elected.
  • In indirect elections, candidates who have been voted by a majority of the members are elected.
  • Election cost will be paid from the national treasury.
  • In the next year subtopics further we’ll be understanding more about the Chinese system its legislative and executive systems.

Military System of Government

Video Part 2 · starts at 6:45:55

🎯 Learning Objectives

  • That is at the end of this session, you will be able to define the concept of military system of government.
  • You will also be able to describe military dictatorship definition and characteristics of military jars.
  • You’ll also be able to describe military versus civilian dictatorships and examples of military dictatorship in the 20th century.

What Is a Military System of Government?

  • In a few earlier subtopics we read about the electoral system of China but in this particular subtopic we’ll be learning and understanding about the concept of military system of government.
  • Now what is the concept of military system of government?
  • Military administration manages the occupied territory by means such as the exercise of administrative, legislative and judicial powers.
  • The need for a military junta arises from the inability of a legitimate government to exercise its function.
  • Here military junda must be distinguished from military law and martial law.

Military Law vs Martial Law

  • Military law is a code that regulates the actions of members of the armed forces but does not apply to civilians.
  • Martial law is if necessary is a temporary government of citizen through an army without the authority of written law and invoked only on domestic territories.
  • Now what is the definition of military dictatorship and its various characteristics?
  • In a military dictatorship, military leaders have substantial or complete control over the people and functions of the government.
  • As a form of dictatorship, a military dictatorship is either a single military junta with unlimited power or a group of high-ranking military officers who can limit the power of a dictator to some extent.
  • In most cases, the military dictatorship comes to power after the former civilian government was overthrown in a coupe.
  • Military dictators usually disband the civilian government altogether.
  • Occasionally components of the civilian government structure may be restored after a coup but are tightly controlled by the military.

Suspension of Constitutional Rights

  • In addition to the suspension or revocation of constitutional rights and freedoms, the almost universal feature of the military dictatorship is a permanent martial law or national emergency aimed at always distracting people for fear of attack.
  • The military junta usually ignores human rights and goes to the extreme to silence political opposition.
  • Ironically, military dictators have often justified their rules as a way to protect people from harmful political ideologies.
  • Based on the general assumption that the military is politically neutral, the military dictatorship may seek to portray themselves as a savior of people from corrupt and exploitative civilian politicians.
  • Military dictatorships often leave as they entered through real or imminent groups or popular rebellions as their oppressive dominant style often gives rise to public descent. Now what are military juntas?

Military Juntas

  • Military junta is a coordinated group of high-ranking military officers who exercise authoritarian or totalitarian control over a country after gaining power by force.
  • Unlike a single dictator or a pure military dictatorship where the power of the military junta is unlimited.
  • Military dictatorship officers can limit the power of dictator.
  • Military junta may choose to control a more limited range of territories such as foreign policy and national security rather than all functions of the country.
  • Further, unlike military dictators, junta leaders can end martial law, dress in civilian clothing, and appoint former military officers to maintain de factor control over local governments and parties. Military versus civilian dictatorships.
  • In contrast to the military dictatorship, the civilian dictatorship is a formal dictatorship that does not draw power directly from the military.
  • Unlike the military dictatorship, the private dictatorship does not have built-in access to organized support infrastructure like the military.

Private Dictators vs Military Rule

  • Instead, private dictators remain in power by controlling the dominant political parties and election process or by gaining public support at a fanatic level.
  • Rather than military threats to create cultlike support and nationalist sentiment among people, charismatic civilian dictators use techniques such as exaggerated propaganda. Mass distribution and psychological warfare.
  • Private dictatorships that rely on political control tend to last longer than personal cutbacked dictatorships.
  • Now the example of military dictatorship in the 20th century is of that of Thailand.
  • On May 22nd 2014, Thailand’s interim government was overthrown in a bloodless coup led by General Prayut Chan-o-cha commander of the Royal Thai Army.

Thailand’s Junta

  • Payut established the military junta.
  • The national council for peace and order to govern the country.
  • Military junta abolished the constitution proclaimed martial law and banned all forms of political expression.
  • In 2017, NCPO issued a provisional constitution, granted almost full power, established a puppet parliament that unanimously elected Prime Minister Prayut. The next example is that of Brazil.
  • From 1964 to 1985, Brazil was dominated by authoritarian military dictatorship.
  • After taking part in a coupe, the commander of the Brazilian army backed by anti-communist interests including the United States enacted a new constitution that limits freedom of speech and outlaws political opposition.
  • The Janata gained general support by encouraging nationalism. Another example is that of Chile.
  • On September 17th, 1973, Chile’s socialist government Salvador Allende was overthrown in a coup backed by the United States.

Argentina, Greece & Chile

  • Over the next 17 years, a military ja led by General Augusto Pinochet organized the most brutal period of human rights abuses in Chile’s history.
  • Chile returned to democracy in 1990, but people continue to suffer from the impact of Pinochet’s military dictatorship.
  • The next country being Argentina after defeating President Eel person deart on March 24th, 1976.
  • A right-wing military officer ruled Argentina until democracy was restored in December 1983. Then comes Greece.
  • From 1967 to 1974, Greece was governed by a farright military dictatorship known as colonials Administration.
  • Their actions were so swift and brutal that by September 1967, the European Commission of Human Rights had charged the colonials administration with several serious human rights abuses.

📌 Session Recap

Thus, in this session, you’ve learned about the concept of military system of government, military dictatorships, characteristics and military gents, military versus civilian dictatorships, and examples of military dictatorship.
